  ["RELIANCE.NS", "ARIMA+GARCH", "0.3836", "0.3637", "-0.0199"],
  ["RELIANCE.NS", "Ensemble-Stack", "0.4233", "0.4029", "-0.0204"],
  ["RELIANCE.NS", "XGBoost", "0.8379", "0.8126", "-0.0253"],
]));
children.push(P([B("Maximum movement: 0.025. "), T("No verdict changes at the 0.05 or 0.10 thresholds. ONE changes at 0.20 - TCS ARIMA+GARCH crosses from 0.1920 to 0.2155, dropping that configuration from Medium to Low, again in the conservative direction. So the fix is correct and necessary, and NO CONCLUSION IN THIS DOCUMENT CHANGES. The empirical findings, the SPA verdict and the “no confirmed winner” framing all stand.")]));

children.push(H3("The Windows race the fix uncovered"));
children.push(P("Re-running the suite after the change produced an intermittent failure - and, tellingly, in a DIFFERENT TEST EACH TIME: three separate drift-report tests failed across runs, each passing in isolation. That pattern says shared non-determinism, not a broken assertion. The tests already used isolated temp directories, so it was not file contamination."));
children.push(P([B("The cause was found only after hardening the test harness. "), T("log_forecast swallows every exception on purpose (a logging failure must never break a forecast), which in a test converts a transient write failure into a SHORT LOG, surfacing several assertions later as an unexplained coverage number. Asserting that every write succeeded turned the puzzle into a named failure, and the named failure pointed at _rewrite: PermissionError [WinError 5] Access is denied, on os.replace of predictions.jsonl.tmp.")]));
children.push(P([B("This is a production bug, not a test artefact. "), T("os.replace is atomic on POSIX; on Windows it raises whenever another process holds even a momentary handle on the destination - an antivirus scanner or the search indexer touching a just-written file is enough. It is a race, failing roughly one run in ten. resolve_outcomes is what run_drift_report.py --resolve calls on the real log, so hitting this would crash the resolve and leave a stray .tmp beside the log. Fixed with retry-and-exponential-backoff - the standard Windows-safe pattern - and the temporary file is removed if every attempt fails, so a failed resolve cannot leave litter a later run mistakes for data.")]));
children.push(P("The general lesson is the one this project keeps rediscovering: the deliberate swallow that makes a system robust in production is the same swallow that makes it undiagnosable in testing. The fix is not to remove it, but to make the test harness refuse to tolerate it."));

children.push(H2("3.39 Universe expansion: point-in-time data, and the first positive result"));
children.push(P("Ten negative results in this project point at the same suspect. The cross-sectional null in Section 3.30 - rank IC +0.0111 at t = 0.92 - was read at the time as a POWER result rather than evidence against the approach: real cross-sectional ICs are 0.02-0.05, and detecting one needs roughly 55-60 validation names where 39 were available. Section 3.32 deferred to the same wall. Breadth was the one lever never pulled. This section pulls it - and most of the work turned out to be in the DATA SOURCE, not the experiment."));

children.push(H3("The obvious source is a trap"));
children.push(P("NSE publishes EQUITY_L.csv, and get_data.get_bse_nse_data already fetches it: 2,565 listings, 2,302 with SERIES == EQ, and 1,154 listed early enough to have full history. Ample to draw 400 names from. It is also A LIST OF COMPANIES THAT STILL EXIST. Measured against Wayback Machine snapshots of the identical URL - the current archives.nseindia.com domain has only two, but the pre-2020 nseindia.com URL has 33 going back to 2006:"));
children.push(table([2600, 3200, 4060], [
  ["Snapshot", "EQ names then", "Absent from today's list"],
  ["2017-07", "1,465", "392 (26.8%)"],
  ["2018-12", "1,456", "306 (21.0%)"],
  ["2021-05", "1,563", "209 (13.4%)"],
]));
children.push(P([B("A quarter of the 2017 universe is gone. "), T("Drawing a 2018-2026 panel from today's roster selects companies ON THE BASIS OF HAVING SURVIVED THE WINDOW, then asks a model to predict their returns over it. A larger biased sample is worse than a small honest one, because it produces a confident answer. The gap cannot be patched from the existing feed: of 30 randomly sampled vanished symbols fetched from yfinance, 23 returned no data at all, 6 partial, 1 usable. yfinance discards delisted history.")]));
children.push(P([B("This is not hypothetical for this project. "), T("TATAMOTORS.NS - one of the 40 names in DEFAULT_UNIVERSE - now returns ZERO BARS from yfinance after the 2025 demerger, while bhavcopy still carries all 490 of its 2018-2019 sessions. That is precisely why the published cross-sectional work ran on 39 usable names rather than 40. The bias had already started eating the universe.")]));

children.push(H3("Bhavcopy: point-in-time by construction"));
children.push(P("NSE publishes a DAILY BHAVCOPY - one file per trading day listing every instrument that traded. There is no membership list to get wrong and no survivorship correction to apply: whatever traded on 2 January 2018 is in the 2 January 2018 file. 3IINFOTECH, ADANITRANS, ADLABS and ADHUNIK are all there with full OHLCV."));
children.push(P("bhavcopy.py is that loader. Two formats, found by probing rather than assumed: the legacy zipped CSV serves 2017-01 through 2024-07-01 and 404s from 2024-07-08; UDiFF via nselib serves 2024-01 onward. fetch_day tries legacy and falls back, so the cutover is absorbed rather than hardcoded. The six-month overlap is a FREE CROSS-VALIDATION, and test_readers_agree_on_the_normalised_schema pins that downstream code cannot tell which source produced a row. The cache is one gzipped file per day - 2,376 trading days plus 145 holidays, ~105 MB, 62 minutes once. A missing day is stored as an EMPTY file, because a weekend and an exchange holiday are indistinguishable from date arithmetic and must be discovered, never interpolated."));

children.push(H3("The harder half: raw prices"));
children.push(P("Bhavcopy carries RAW TRADED PRICES. yfinance was silently handling splits and bonuses; bhavcopy does not, and an unadjusted 1:10 split reads as a -90% return that would poison every momentum feature in the panel. PREVCLOSE is no help - checked on seven 2019 split/bonus ex-dates it equals the prior day's raw close exactly (ratio 1.000). NSE does not adjust it either. So the adjustment is ours, parsed from nselib's corporate-action feed (coverage verified back to January 2017):"));
children.push(table([5600, 4260], [
  ["Subject line", "Adjustment"],
  ["Bonus 1:4", "x1.25"],
  ["Face Value Split From Rs 10/- ... To Rs 2/- ...", "x5"],
  ["Bonus 1:2/Face Value Split ... Rs 2/- To Re 1/-", "x3 (compound)"],
  ["Interim Dividend Rs 2/- Per Share", "Rs 2 cash"],
  ["Rights 19:67 @ Premium Rs 215 Per Share", "via TERP"],
]));
children.push(P("Dividends are included because the pipeline models log returns off adj_close (get_data ~line 352), yfinance's FULLY back-adjusted series. Adjusting only for splits would make the new panel incomparable with every figure already published here."));
children.push(P([B("A bug caught during validation. "), T("GPIL filed a 10->5 split AND a 1:1 bonus as two separate records on 2021-10-26 - true factor 4.0. An early version deduplicated by (symbol, ex_date) and parsed 2.0, an 82% error. Same-day actions must COMPOUND; test_same_day_actions_compound exists so it cannot return. Every correction is expressed as the ratio of theoretical post-event price to cum price, so compounding is correct by construction rather than by patch.")]));

children.push(H3("Validated against yfinance, which found the one thing left out"));
children.push(P("A per-event check (does the price drop by the parsed factor?) is confounded by the stock's genuine move that day - which is why one of 18 hand-checked events came in 13% off with a perfectly correct factor. The stronger test compares the WHOLE RETURN SERIES against an independent adjusted source. run_bhavcopy_audit.py does that over 2018-2019:"));
children.push(table([4600, 5260], [
  ["Measure", "Result"],
  ["Median return correlation", "1.00000"],
  ["Names above 0.999", "24 / 24"],
  ["Names bit-identical", "14"],
  ["Worst single-day gap", "0.83%"],
]));
children.push(P([B("It did not start there. "), T("The first run gave 22/24, with BHARTIARTL (0.98507) and TATASTEEL (0.99537) failing - and both turned out to be RIGHTS ISSUES, which the parser did not handle. Adding theoretical-ex-rights-price adjustment moved them to 0.99986 and 0.99999. Worth recording: NSE quotes the PREMIUM OVER FACE VALUE, not the subscription price, so Bharti's 'Rs 215' is really Rs 220 against a Rs 5 face value, and the face value has to come from the feed's faceVal column. Tata Steel's subject carries a fully-paid AND a partly-paid tranche; only the first is taken, because a partly-paid share is a separate instrument and folding it in would be wrong in a worse way than ignoring it.")]));
children.push(P("adjust_prices(as_of=...) applies ONLY actions known by that date, which closes the back-adjustment look-ahead Section 3.19 documented and yfinance cannot avoid."));

children.push(H3("The experiment"));
children.push(P("run_universe_expansion.py runs the IDENTICAL experiment at four universe sizes - same features, labels, split rule, model and horizon. Only breadth varies. The integration is one function, deliberately: cross_sectional_panel.build_panel already accepted an injected fetch_fn so its tests could run offline, and bhavcopy.bhav_fetcher slots into that seam. NOT ONE LINE of cross_sectional_panel.py, cross_sectional_model.py or the feature code changed. Had the pipeline been rebuilt alongside the data source, no comparison with the published 39-name result would have meant anything."));
children.push(P("Window 2018-01-01 to 2024-06-30, horizon 5 days, mode='combined' (unseen names AND unseen dates), seed 42:"));
children.push(table([1300, 1400, 1700, 1500, 1800, 1200, 1060], [
  ["Requested", "Usable", "Val names", "IC dates", "Rank IC", "FM t", "Abstain"],
  ["39", "39", "12", "464", "+0.0283", "1.31", "0.968"],
  ["100", "100", "30", "464", "+0.0048", "0.24", "0.956"],
  ["200", "197", "59", "464", "+0.0375", "2.37", "0.937"],
  ["400", "332", "100", "464", "+0.0396", "2.24", "0.922"],
]));
children.push(P("The 39-name row reproduces the project's own history - t = 1.31 against the published 0.92 - on a different window and a differently-selected universe. That is the control."));
children.push(P([B("Re-measured in the full audit (Section 3.60). "), T("The table above used the 30-feature set; Section 3.56 removed bb_position (an exact duplicate of price_zscore / 2), which changes LightGBM\'s column sampling, and the table was never re-run. With the shipped 29 features and seed 42 the rows read: 39 names IC +0.0068 (t 0.34), 100 names -0.0063 (-0.29), 200 names +0.0378 (2.30), 400 names +0.0373 (t 2.12). Restoring bb_position reproduces the 39-, 100- and 200-name rows exactly; the 400-name row also moves with live corporate-action revisions. The seed study reproduces - median IC +0.0372, median t +2.40, 7 of 8 seeds above 2, none negative - so the finding stands on the robust measure. Re-run on a frozen copy of the feed (Section 3.74), with renamed companies\' corporate actions now included (Section 3.75), it reads median IC +0.0392, median t +2.58, 7 of 8 above 2, none negative; the feed is no longer fetched afresh on every run.")]));

children.push(H3("It was not reported until it survived being attacked"));
children.push(P([B("The table is not monotonic. "), T("Under a pure power story t should climb roughly with the square root of the validation count; instead 1.31 -> 0.24 -> 2.37 -> 2.24. The dip at 100 is larger than sampling noise should comfortably allow, and every row is ONE SEED. Section 3.30 already recorded a case where one seed gave a reportable t = 2.43 where the honest read gave 1.69.")]));
children.push(P("run_universe_seeds.py builds the panel once (it does not depend on the seed) and re-splits, re-fits and re-evaluates, then repeats the whole thing on two DISJOINT periods:"));
children.push(table([2700, 1300, 1900, 1700, 1200, 1160], [
  ["Window", "IC dates", "Rank IC median", "FM t median", "t > 2", "IC < 0"],
  ["Full 2018-01 .. 2024-06", "464", "+0.0367", "+2.42", "7 / 8", "0 / 8"],
  ["A: 2018-01 .. 2021-06", "~230", "+0.0336", "+1.92", "2 / 6", "0 / 6"],
  ["B: 2021-07 .. 2024-12", "240", "+0.0328", "+2.70", "6 / 6", "0 / 6"],
]));
children.push(P([B("Twenty of twenty seed-runs are positive, "), T("and the effect SIZE is near-identical across two non-overlapping periods: 0.0367 / 0.0336 / 0.0328. Period A's weaker t is not a weaker effect - its IC is the same size, measured over half the dates with more than double the dispersion (sd 0.0145 vs 0.0065), which is what a window containing the COVID crash should look like. So the n = 100 dip was a single-draw artefact, and THE CROSS-SECTIONAL NULL IN SECTION 3.30 WAS A POWER RESULT EXACTLY AS CLAIMED. This is the project's FIRST POSITIVE RESULT after ten negatives.")]));

children.push(H3("What this result is not"));
children.push(P([B("1. The long-short spread is not usable; the rank IC is. "), T("The spread looks large (+0.017 to +0.061 of 5-day excess log return) and rests on almost nothing:")]));
children.push(table([2400, 2400, 2500, 2560], [
  ["Universe", "Long calls", "Short calls", "of predictions"],
  ["39", "0", "143", "4,405"],
  ["100", "26", "499", "11,813"],
  ["200", "18", "1,573", "25,085"],
  ["400", "148", "2,978", "39,844"],
]));
children.push(P("148 longs out of 39,844 is 0.37%, against 20x as many shorts. That is not a balanced portfolio, the mean of 148 observations is dominated by a handful of moves, and no transaction costs appear anywhere in it. At n = 39 there were ZERO long calls, which is why that spread is undefined. Quote the IC, not the spread."));
children.push(P([B("2. The classifier still barely classifies. "), T("Accuracy 0.510 against a 0.501 majority baseline; macro F1 0.288. The rank IC carries the entire result - the model ORDERS names better than chance while its discrete calls stay close to worthless. Abstention holds at 92% even at 400 names, so Section 3.30's emergent-abstention behaviour is not a small-sample artefact. Calibration is genuinely good, though: ECE 0.0204 -> 0.0026.")]));
children.push(P([B("3. A residual survivorship filter remains, smaller but real. "), T("build_panel requires 300 rows per name, so companies trading for under ~14 months inside the window are dropped - 68 of 400 at the largest size. And a company that fails simply stops appearing; its final observed return is not -100%, so genuine wipeouts are under-represented. Both push optimistic. Far weaker than 'must still exist in 2026', and not zero.")]));
children.push(P([B("4. One market, one model family, gross of costs. "), T("No capacity analysis, and no borrow cost on a book that is 95% short by count.")]));
children.push(H3("What the signal is made of - and why the model is not the finding"));
children.push(P("A statistical result whose mechanism is unknown is one step away from an artefact nobody has found yet. run_universe_attribution.py asks what the 400-name model is actually keying on, with the decisive measurement being the UNIVARIATE IC: for every raw feature, the Fama-MacBeth IC of that feature ALONE against forward excess return - same rows, same 464 dates, same machinery as the model."));
children.push(table([2400, 2100, 1500, 1800, 2060], [
  ["Feature", "Univariate IC", "FM t", "Hit rate", "SHAP share"],
  ["log_return_5", "-0.0524", "-4.34", "0.388", "1.6%"],
  ["rank_rev_5", "-0.0524", "-4.34", "0.388", "4.2%"],
  ["ma_ratio_10", "-0.0491", "-4.01", "0.386", "1.6%"],
  ["macd_hist_norm", "-0.0470", "-3.68", "0.373", "3.1%"],
  ["log_return_3", "-0.0442", "-4.08", "0.397", "0.9%"],
  ["rank_mom_63", "+0.0276", "+2.24", "0.595", "7.1%"],
  ["the model", "+0.0396", "+2.24", "0.565", "-"],
]));
children.push(P([B("A single raw feature beats the model - 1.32x the IC and nearly double the t (1.41x against the shipped 29-feature model, Section 3.60). "), T("And the sign is unambiguous: a negative IC on recent returns means recent winners underperform, which is SHORT-TERM REVERSAL, one of the most documented effects in equities. The hit rate of 0.388 says the IC is negative on 61% of dates, so it is consistent rather than carried by a handful of them. The whole top of the table is the same effect wearing different names.")]));
children.push(P([B("A second textbook effect appears with the correct opposite sign. "), T("rank_mom_63 scores +0.0276 at t = +2.24 - positive IC at a 63-day lookback. Short-horizon reversal AND medium-horizon momentum, both present, both correctly signed, in a survivorship-free panel built from scratch. That is a strong independent check on the entire data pipeline: if the bhavcopy loader or the corporate-action adjustment were subtly wrong, these two effects would not both emerge at their textbook signs and horizons.")]));
children.push(P([B("The SHAP explains why the model underperforms. "), T("Its top three features by attribution are volatility_50 (14.8%), atr_norm (9.0%) and rank_vol_21 (7.3%) - all volatility. rank_rev_5, which carries the actual edge, ranks SEVENTH at 4.2%. The model spends its capacity deciding WHEN TO STAY OUT rather than WHICH WAY TO LEAN, which is coherent with the 92% abstention and is exactly what it was punished for.")]));
children.push(P([B("This also reverses Section 3.36's reading. "), T("Attribution there was near-uniform (Herfindahl 1.3x uniform) and was recorded as 'no driver to look at'. At 400 names it concentrates: the top feature takes 14.8% where uniform would be ~3%. THERE WAS A DRIVER; THE 39-NAME UNIVERSE WAS HIDING IT, in the same way and for the same reason it hid the IC.")]));
children.push(H3("The honest summary"));
children.push(P("There is a REAL, REPLICABLE cross-sectional ordering signal in liquid NSE equities at a 5-day horizon, invisible at 39 names and detectable at 200+. That part of Section 3.30's prediction is confirmed."));
children.push(P([B("But the model is not the finding - the factor is. "), T("This section is a known effect REDISCOVERED, not learned cross-sectional skill, and a naive 5-day reversal sort is the benchmark any model here has to beat. This one does not beat it. Anyone reporting the +0.0396 without the -0.0524 beside it would be overstating what the machine learning contributed, which is: nothing yet.")]));
children.push(P("That is a better outcome than an unexplained edge. An edge you cannot explain is one you cannot reason about when it stops working - and short-term reversal has a known mechanism (liquidity provision, bid-ask bounce, over-reaction), known decay, and known capacity limits. Whether it survives costs is answered in Section 3.40: IT DOES NOT. Breakeven is 5.0 bp round trip against an STT of 20 bp, and no holding period or tail width in the sweep clears even 25 bp."));

children.push(H2("3.40 Costing the reversal signal - the eleventh negative result, and the end of the arc"));
children.push(P("Section 3.39 closed with an open question: the cross-sectional signal is real, replicable and identified as short-term reversal, but 'whether it survives costs is a different question this project has not asked.' This asks it, and the answer closes the arc."));
children.push(P("run_reversal_costs.py builds the tradeable version: long the bottom decile of 5-day past return (recent losers), short the top decile (recent winners), equal-weighted, dollar-neutral, rebalanced every 5 trading days so holding periods do not overlap. 339 names, 1,601 dates, 319 rebalances. The signal is a cross-sectional sort on a raw feature with NO FREE PARAMETERS - nothing is fitted - so the whole 2018-2024 panel is fair game and there is no train/test split to respect."));
children.push(H3("It was already thin before costs"));
children.push(table([5000, 4860], [
  ["Measure", "Gross (no costs)"],
  ["Annual return", "+4.40%"],
  ["Annual volatility", "22.49%"],
  ["Sharpe", "+0.20"],
  ["Periods positive", "0.511"],
]));
children.push(P([B("An IC of -0.052 sounds respectable and converts to a Sharpe of 0.20. "), T("The decile spread carries 22% annualised volatility, so the risk-adjusted edge was small before a single rupee of cost. Section 3.39's statistical significance came from having 464 dates, not from a large effect - which is exactly the distinction the Fama-MacBeth machinery exists to expose, and it cuts both ways.")]));
children.push(H3("Costs remove it several times over"));
children.push(P("The book turns over 3.47 of a possible 4.00 notional per rebalance - an 87% refresh, every five days."));
children.push(table([2000, 2200, 1500, 4160], [
  ["Round trip", "Annual return", "Sharpe", "Scenario"],
  ["10 bp", "-4.34%", "-0.19", "institutional, futures, top liquidity"],
  ["25 bp", "-17.46%", "-0.78", "liquid cash equity, modest size"],
  ["50 bp", "-39.32%", "-1.75", "realistic incl. mid-cap impact"],
  ["100 bp", "-83.05%", "-3.70", "retail delivery, STT both sides"],
]));
children.push(P([B("Breakeven: 5.0 bp round trip. "), T("That is the number to argue with, because it assumes nothing - it is simply where gross return equals cost drag. For scale, STT ALONE IS 20 bp ROUND TRIP on Indian delivery, before brokerage, exchange fees, stamp duty or market impact.")]));
children.push(P([B("Later correction: this book is the luckiest of five equally valid versions. "), T("A 5-day book rebalanced every 5 sessions can start on any of 5 days. Gross per rebalance is +0.087%, +0.016%, -0.100%, -0.053% and +0.070% across them; the +0.087% reported here (Sharpe 0.20) is the best of the five, because the loop happened to start on row 5. Averaged over all five, gross is +0.004% and no starting day reaches |t| = 1. The verdict only strengthens: the raw reversal book is indistinguishable from zero before costs. See Section 3.50.")]));
children.push(H3("The obvious rescue does not work, and the reason is interesting"));
children.push(P("A turnover-driven null invites one objection: rebalance more slowly. Tested across holding periods and tail widths:"));
children.push(table([1200, 1200, 2200, 1900, 1500, 1860], [
  ["Hold", "Tail", "Notional traded", "Gross annual", "Sharpe", "Breakeven"],
  ["5d", "1/10", "3.470", "+4.40%", "+0.20", "5.0 bp"],
  ["5d", "1/5", "3.159", "+3.57%", "+0.22", "4.5 bp"],
  ["10d", "1/10", "3.472", "-1.60%", "-0.08", "-3.7 bp"],
  ["21d", "1/10", "3.485", "-2.42%", "-0.14", "-11.6 bp"],
  ["42d", "1/10", "3.456", "-9.15%", "-0.55", "-88.3 bp"],
]));
children.push(P([B("Only the 5-day book has a positive gross return at all. "), T("At 10 days and beyond the effect is gone and the sign flips - consistent with the reversal literature, where the effect lives at days-to-weeks and decays fast.")]));
children.push(P("(Re-run in Section 3.60. The headline 5-day row reproduces exactly; the longer holds move in the second decimal because build_panel re-fetches NSE\'s corporate-action feed live and revisions shift adjusted prices slightly. Later: the feed is now frozen (Section 3.74), and every row was re-run after Section 3.75\'s fix, which is where the 5.0 bp now comes from.)"));
children.push(P([B("The unexpected part is the middle column: turnover barely moves. "), T("Holding for 42 days instead of 5 still trades ~3.46 of 4.00. That is because the signal has NO PERSISTENCE - a 5-day return is close to uncorrelated with the 5-day return measured 42 days later, so decile membership is redrawn essentially from scratch at every rebalance regardless of spacing. Slowing down therefore buys NO turnover reduction while forfeiting all of the return. That is the mechanism, and it is why this particular null cannot be engineered around: the horizon at which the signal exists and the horizon at which it would be cheap to trade are disjoint.")]));
children.push(H3("A second, independent blocker"));
children.push(P([B("Indian cash equity has no overnight short selling. "), T("A 5-day short leg needs single-stock futures, and those exist for only 131 of the 339 universe names (39%) - on TODAY'S F&O list, which is an upper bound since the list was narrower historically. So the short side - where Section 3.39's model made 95% of its calls - is unimplementable for nearly two-thirds of the universe. Restricting to F&O names would cut breadth by that much, and breadth is precisely what generated the IC in the first place. Two independent reasons for failure is a sturdier conclusion than one.")]));
children.push(H3("The arc, complete"));
children.push(table([2200, 7660], [
  ["Stage", "Finding"],
  ["Section 3.30", "No cross-sectional skill at 39 names (t = 0.92) - diagnosed as UNDERPOWERED, not absent"],
  ["Section 3.39", "At 400 survivorship-free names the signal IS there: IC +0.0396, t = 2.24, 20/20 seeds"],
  ["Section 3.39 attribution", "It is SHORT-TERM REVERSAL - and one raw feature beats the model"],
  ["Section 3.40", "It is NOT TRADEABLE: breakeven 5.0 bp, no configuration clears 25 bp"],
]));
children.push(P([B("The eleventh negative result, and the one that completes the story rather than deferring it. "), T("The honest summary of this project's central question is now a single sentence: there is a small, real, statistically solid cross-sectional signal in liquid NSE equities, it is a long-known effect rather than anything learned, and it is worth less than the cost of trading it.")]));
children.push(P("That is a more useful place to stop than an unresolved positive. A statistically significant result left uncosted would have been the most misleading thing this repository could ship."));

children.push(H2("3.41 Range-based volatility (Sharma & Mehta Ch. 18) - the twelfth negative result"));
children.push(P("Deep Learning Tools for Predicting Stock Market Movements (Sharma & Mehta, eds., 2024) is mostly surveys, bibliometrics and questionnaire-based behavioural studies - Chapters 4, 7, 8, 10-15 and 17 contain nothing implementable, Chapters 2 and 5 are quantum computing, and Chapter 1's LSTM+ARIMA+sentiment stack is already here minus the sentiment feed Section 3.27 rejected as licensed."));
children.push(P([B("Chapter 18 "), T("(Kaur & Chaudhary, 'Volatility Transmission Role of Indian Equity and Commodity Markets') is the exception, and its useful contribution is NOT the Diebold-Yilmaz spillover model in the title. It is one line of methodology: sigma^2 = 0.361 * [ln(P_high) - ln(P_low)]^2, with the note that 'literature has proven the advantage of using a range-based volatility estimator as compared to the traditional method'. That constant is 1/(4 ln 2) = 0.3607 - the PARKINSON (1980) estimator.")]));

children.push(H3("The gap it pointed at"));
children.push(P("HAR_RV_model builds its variance series as RV_t = r_t^2, and its own docstring calls that 'the standard proxy WHEN SUB-BAR DATA IS UNAVAILABLE'. Sub-bar data is not unavailable: every bar the app fetches carries open, high and low, used for features and never for the variance estimate that drives the uncertainty cone - which this document names as the actual product. The motivating case is stark: a bar that opens at 100, runs to 108, falls to 94 and closes at 100 has r_t = 0 and therefore ZERO estimated variance. The range saw all of it."));
children.push(P("range_volatility.py implements Parkinson, Garman-Klass, Rogers-Satchell and Yang-Zhang. Against a synthetic process with known variance the efficiency gains land on theory: Parkinson 5.07x (theory 5.2), Garman-Klass 6.77x (7.4). On real NSE bars the gain is 1.7-2.4x, NOT 5x - real bars trade discretely, so the observed high and low understate the true extremes. The measured number is the one reported. (First published as 1.6-2.5x: efficiency() compared against squared returns on the RAW close, so a split added a fake return to the baseline and flattered the range estimators - TATASTEEL 2.34x against 1.94x adjusted. Fixed in Section 3.60.)"));

children.push(H3("A trap, hit and then pinned"));
children.push(P([B("Range estimators are within-bar ratios, "), T("so they are invariant to any rescaling of a bar's prices and therefore immune to split and dividend adjustment. Close-to-close returns are not. scale_to_total compares the two, to restore the overnight variance an intraday estimator misses. Measured with RAW closes, TATASTEEL's scale factor came out at 8.32 (level ratio 0.120); with adjusted closes, 1.26 (0.794). Four of six names were corrupted this way - only the two without splits in the window were unaffected. The function now flags implausible factors, and test_scale_to_total_flags_an_unadjusted_close exists so it cannot recur.")]));
children.push(P("The honest level ratios: the intraday range captures 64-85% of close-to-close variance. The rest happens overnight, when earnings and news land - which is why a naive substitution would have understated the cone and caused under-coverage."));

children.push(H3("The A/B, and two corrections to the analysis"));
children.push(P("12 names, 24 walk-forward origins, horizon 5, measuring COVERAGE AND WIDTH TOGETHER - width alone is meaningless because the conformal layer rescales the cone to target and would absorb a constant bias in the variance estimate."));
children.push(table([2600, 2400, 1600, 1900, 1360], [
  ["Estimator", "Mean width delta", "sd", "Paired t", "Wins"],
  ["parkinson", "+0.48%", "5.51%", "0.30", "6/12"],
  ["garman_klass", "-0.17%", "5.44%", "-0.11", "6/12"],
  ["rogers_satchell", "-1.11%", "5.87%", "-0.65", "7/12"],
]));
children.push(P("Every |t| < 1, win rates are coin flips, and the per-ticker spread runs -8.5% to +10%. NO ESTIMATOR CHANGES THE CONE. Getting there required correcting the work twice, and both corrections are the point."));
children.push(P("(Re-run in Section 3.60. The original 12 names were never recorded, so the driver\'s default is now an explicit list - RELIANCE, TCS, INFY, HDFCBANK, SBIN, TATASTEEL, ICICIBANK, ITC, LT, AXISBANK, MARUTI, WIPRO - at 24 origins: parkinson +0.54% (paired t +0.37), garman_klass -0.05% (-0.03), rogers_satchell -1.13% (-0.71), coverage ~0.921 for every arm. Same verdict. The driver also only checked for a significantly TIGHTER cone, so a significantly WIDER one - its old 6-name, 8-origin default gave t = +2.3 - printed \'every paired t is inside +/-2\'; both directions are now reported.)"));
children.push(P([B("1. The first run was underpowered and said the opposite. "), T("At 8 origins the range estimators looked 8-12% WORSE; at 24 origins the sign flipped to 1.4% BETTER. A result that reverses when power is added was never a result. Section 3.30 published an underpowered null that Section 3.39 later overturned; applying that lesson to THIS measurement rather than only to the older ones is what caught it.")]));
children.push(P([B("2. The verdict logic was over-claiming. "), T("It declared 'rogers_satchell wins at -1.4% width' from a paired t of -0.65 - precisely the error the rest of this project exists to prevent, written into the new driver. Replaced with a PAIRED test that compares each ticker against itself, so the large between-name variation is differenced out instead of averaged into a decisive-looking mean, and that requires |t| > 2 before naming a winner.")]));

children.push(H3("Why it fails - a more precise measurement of the wrong quantity"));
children.push(P("scale_to_total restores the missing overnight variance with a CONSTANT factor, but the intraday/overnight split is not constant - it moves with the news and earnings cycle. The range estimators therefore trade sampling noise for a time-varying bias. They are genuinely better at measuring the LAST bar's variance (2x more precise, measured) and no better at forecasting the NEXT one, which is the only thing the cone needs. That distinction is the finding, and it generalises: an estimator's efficiency at describing history is not the same property as its usefulness in a forecast."));
children.push(P("range_volatility.py ships complete, tested (18 tests) and wired into run_har_rv_prediction(rv_estimator=...). The shipped default stays 'squared', per this project's standing rule that an unvalidated change does not ship."));
children.push(P("A note on the coverage column: every arm, baseline included, covers ~0.919 against a 0.95 target in this walk-forward configuration. That is a property of the configuration, not of the estimator choice, and it does not affect the comparison - but it is worth not glossing."));

children.push(H2("3.42 Open interest, and a look-ahead worth 2.9 sigma - the thirteenth negative result"));
children.push(P("Every negative result before this one was measured on a TRANSFORMATION OF OHLCV: more models, more architectures, fractional differencing, range volatility, cross-sectional ranks. Section 3.33 was the single attempt at a second data axis and it died on COVERAGE, not on evidence."));
children.push(P([B("Open interest is the first genuinely orthogonal, obtainable data source this project has found. "), T("It measures POSITIONING - how many contracts are open - and is not derived from price at all. nselib.derivatives.future_price_volume_data serves it historically and free. So the question is different in kind from 'does another function of price help', which has now been answered twelve times.")]));

children.push(H3("The claim, reduced"));
children.push(P("Chapter 3 (Bakshi, 'Analyzing Open Interest') gives a 2x2 interpretation table with NO backtest, NO statistics and NO validation anywhere in the chapter:"));
children.push(table([1800, 1800, 6260], [
  ["Price", "OI", "The chapter's claim"],
  ["Rising", "Rising", "Strongly bullish; trend continues"],
  ["Rising", "Falling", "Short-covering rally; downtrend next"],
  ["Falling", "Rising", "Aggressive short-selling; bearish continues"],
  ["Falling", "Falling", "Forced liquidation; upward move may start"],
]));
children.push(P("Read as a predicted sign, that table is exactly signal = sign(delta price) x sign(delta OI): +1 when the two agree, -1 when they diverge. The whole chapter collapses to one product of two signs, which is convenient - it turns four rules into a single testable signal. Tested on 60 liquid F&O names over 2023-2024 (479 dates), with delta OI from the NEAR-MONTH contract and both delta price and the forward return from the survivorship-free, corporate-action-adjusted bhavcopy panel of Section 3.39."));

children.push(H3("It looked real"));
children.push(table([3200, 3200, 3460], [
  ["Horizon", "Mean IC", "FM t"],
  ["1 day", "+0.0198", "+2.88"],
  ["5 day", "+0.0078", "+1.12"],
]));
children.push(P("At one day that clears the conventional threshold, on a genuinely orthogonal data source, with all four quadrants leaning the direction the chapter predicts. It would have been the project's second positive finding."));

children.push(H3("It was a publication-timing look-ahead"));
children.push(P([B("NSE publishes the F&O bhavcopy - and therefore open interest for date t - AFTER the close of t. "), T("You cannot trade at the close of t on a number that does not exist until afterwards. The earliest possible entry is the close of t+1, so the honest forward return runs from t+1.")]));
children.push(table([5200, 2400, 2260], [
  ["Variant", "Mean IC", "FM t"],
  ["1d, trade at close t - NOT tradeable", "+0.0198", "+2.88"],
  ["1d, TRADEABLE (one-day lag)", "-0.0018", "-0.27"],
  ["5d, trade at close t", "+0.0078", "+1.12"],
  ["5d, TRADEABLE", "+0.0004", "+0.05"],
]));
children.push(P([B("The effect does not decay under the lag - it vanishes, "), T("and flips sign. Out of period it is nothing in both halves: 2023 t = +0.11, 2024 t = -0.50. The entire 2.9-sigma result was one day of hindsight.")]));

children.push(H3("Why this one is worth a section"));
children.push(P("Nothing here was a modelling choice. The features, the horizon, the universe and the statistic were all fixed before the number appeared; the only thing that changed was WHETHER THE DATA EXISTED WHEN THE TRADE WOULD HAVE BEEN PLACED. A one-day misalignment on a daily signal manufactured an IC of +0.02 at t = 2.88 out of an effect of exactly zero."));
children.push(P("That is a useful calibration on every 'significant' daily-frequency result in this literature, and on the chapter itself - which asserts its table confidently and never tests it at all. The driver now prints BOTH rows, labelled, so the gap is visible rather than reconstructible."));
children.push(P("Two further reasons the tradeable version was never going to survive even if it had held: only 39% of a liquid NSE universe is F&O-eligible (Section 3.40), so breadth is cut by two-thirds; and a ONE-DAY signal implies ~100% daily turnover against the ~20 bp of STT alone that Section 3.40 measured, where a 5-day signal at 5.0 bp breakeven already failed."));
children.push(P([B("The thirteenth negative result, and the first to rule out an orthogonal data source rather than another feature of the same one. "), T("The book's only empirically testable claim does not survive contact with its own publication schedule.")]));

children.push(H2("3.43 Microstructural features (Lopez de Prado, AFML Ch. 19) - a usable feature, an unusable measurement"));
children.push(P("Advances in Financial Machine Learning is a different proposition from the survey volumes in Sections 3.41 and 3.42. Almost everything in it is implementable. The constraint is that most of it needs data this project does not have: Chapter 5 (fractional differentiation) is already done and null in Section 3.37; Chapters 6 and 9 are covered by Section 3.28; Chapter 2's tick/volume/dollar bars need tick data; Chapter 16 (Hierarchical Risk Parity) is a portfolio product, not this one; Chapters 20-22 are HPC recipes."));
children.push(P([B("A note on Chapters 3 and 4, because it is a trap worth naming. "), T("AFML's sample weights by average uniqueness are EFFECTIVELY - though not exactly - a no-op for this project as it stands, and the distinction is worth getting right rather than asserting the stronger claim. Running AFML's own Snippets 4.1/4.2 on this project's label structure (fixed 5-day horizon, sampled every bar) against a variable-lifespan alternative:")]));
children.push(table([3400, 3300, 3160], [
  ["Measure", "Fixed 5-day horizon", "Triple-barrier (variable)"],
  ["Concurrency", "1 to 6", "0 to 17"],
  ["Uniqueness sd", "0.0219", "0.0259"],
  ["Max/min weight ratio", "2.45", "6.11"],
]));
children.push(P([B("The 2.45x ratio looks like a real effect and is ENTIRELY A BOUNDARY ARTEFACT. "), T("Across the interior, uniqueness is EXACTLY 1/6 with a standard deviation of 0.00e+00; it rises only in the first and last ~5 bars, where fewer labels overlap - the first eight values run 0.4083, 0.2694, 0.2139, 0.1861, 0.1722, 0.1667, 0.1667, 0.1667, and only 10 of 295 labels differ from the interior value (3.4%). On the real cross-sectional panel that is roughly 3,400 of 495,000 rows - 0.68%, all of it at sample edges.")]));
children.push(P("So the weighting only BITES when label lifespans vary, which requires the triple-barrier labels of Chapter 3 first - and triple-barrier more than doubles the weight dispersion (6.11x against 2.45x). They are a package, not two independent techniques, and implementing Chapter 4 alone would have produced an elaborate almost-no-op that looked like rigour."));

children.push(H3("Why Chapter 19 was the pick"));
children.push(P("Section 3.40 concluded that short-term reversal does not survive transaction costs - breakeven 5.0 bp round trip. That conclusion rests on ASSUMED cost scenarios (10/25/50/100 bp), the weakest link in an otherwise measured argument. Corwin & Schultz (2012), via AFML Ch. 19.3.4, estimate the bid-ask spread from HIGH AND LOW PRICES ALONE - data already in hand. If it works, Section 3.40's central assumption becomes a measurement. microstructure.py implements it, along with Roll (1984), Becker-Parkinson volatility, and the Amihud and Kyle impact measures. The rest of Ch. 19 - VPIN, order-flow imbalance, the sequential-trade models - needs tick-level SIGNED VOLUME and is deliberately not approximated."));

children.push(H3("It does not work as a measurement, for two compounding reasons"));
children.push(P([B("1. The estimator is biased upward, in proportion to volatility. "), T("Simulated bars with a true spread of EXACTLY ZERO:")]));
children.push(table([3200, 3400, 3260], [
  ["sigma (daily)", "CS reports", "bias / sigma"],
  ["0.5%", "12.2 bp", "0.244"],
  ["1.0%", "24.4 bp", "0.244"],
  ["2.0%", "48.7 bp", "0.244"],
  ["4.0%", "97.5 bp", "0.244"],
]));
children.push(P("It attributes part of the price range to a spread that is not there. The slope against a GENUINE spread is ~0.93, so the estimator does track real spread almost one-for-one and adds a volatility-sized offset on top. Quoting the raw figure as a transaction cost would OVERSTATE costs - which would have made Section 3.40's negative conclusion look stronger than the evidence supports, the most tempting direction to get something wrong."));
children.push(P([B("2. Correcting the bias against a simulated null fails, because real bars are not GBM. "), T("spread_null calibrates the estimator on zero-spread bars at the series' own volatility - the same permutation-null discipline feature_drift.psi_null uses. On synthetic data it works, recovering a known 50 bp spread to within 12 bp and returning zero when the true spread is zero, erring CONSERVATIVE (it understates by ~20%, because the volatility used to calibrate the null is itself inflated by the spread). On real NSE bars it collapses: the raw estimate (10-38 bp) is BELOW the GBM null (31-48 bp) for 9 of the 10 large caps tested, so nine floor at zero and ITC reads 4 bp. (Corrected in Section 3.60: the table above was one draw - across seeds the bias runs 0.24-0.32 x sigma - and a later fix measured the null on gap-adjusted bars, lowering it. Same conclusion.) Real daily bars differ from a random walk in ways - fat-tailed ranges, intraday mean reversion, discrete trading - that change the estimator's behaviour more than the spread does.")]));

children.push(H3("An omission in AFML's snippet, found by chasing the discrepancy"));
children.push(P("The estimator contrasts a TWO-BAR range (gamma) against the sum of two ONE-BAR ranges (beta). An overnight gap sits inside the two-bar range and inside neither one-bar range, so it inflates gamma alone - pushing alpha down and the spread with it. Measured on NSE bars, gamma/beta runs 1.08 to 2.62 against 1.015 for a gapless random walk."));
children.push(P([B("AFML's Snippet 19.1 omits the correction; the original paper specifies it "), T("(Corwin & Schultz 2012, p. 727): slide a gapped bar to meet the previous close, preserving its height. overnight_adjust implements it and it is on by default. Honest accounting: it moved the estimates by only a few basis points (INFY 16.3 -> 22.3, TATASTEEL 20.7 -> 28.8, most names unchanged) and did NOT rescue the null correction. It is correct, it is required by the source, and it was not the explanation.")]));

children.push(H3("What it IS good for"));
children.push(P("The weaker claim survives. Across 1,392 NSE names:"));
children.push(table([4800, 5060], [
  ["Turnover quintile", "Median CS spread"],
  ["Q1 (thinnest)", "106.9 bp"],
  ["Q2", "88.4 bp"],
  ["Q3", "79.1 bp"],
  ["Q4", "66.8 bp"],
  ["Q5 (most liquid)", "44.3 bp"],
]));
children.push(P([B("Spearman(CS, log turnover) = -0.63, "), T("correctly signed and monotone across every quintile. The estimator ranks liquidity well while getting the level wrong by an order of magnitude - actual quoted spreads on RELIANCE or TCS are ~1-3 bp, not 44. So Corwin-Schultz is a RELATIVE LIQUIDITY FEATURE, not a cost estimate. That is a real if modest result, and it is the opposite of what the section set out to produce.")]));

children.push(H3("Where this leaves Section 3.40"));
children.push(P("Section 3.40's assumed costs CANNOT be replaced by a measured spread through this route, and the section's caveat stands as written. The conclusion is nonetheless SUPPORTED, by a cruder argument than intended: even the most liquid quintile of the entire NSE reads 44 bp on an estimator that is biased upward and still an order of magnitude above a 5.0 bp breakeven. There is no reading of this data - biased, corrected, or raw - under which spreads approach breakeven."));
children.push(P([B("The fourteenth negative result, and the second where a technique that failed its intended purpose turned out to be usable for a smaller one. "), T("(Section 3.41 was the first: range estimators measure the last bar well and forecast the next one no better.) microstructure.py ships with 22 tests, and the shipped cost scenarios in Section 3.40 stay as they are.")]));

children.push(H2("3.44 The Deflated Sharpe Ratio (AFML 14.7) - and what it says about Section 3.40"));
children.push(P("Section 3.43 closed with two AFML techniques worth taking. This is the first, and unlike everything since Section 3.39 it is not a search for signal - it is a SHARPER INSTRUMENT FOR JUDGING RESULTS ALREADY IN HAND. That is the right category for a project carrying fourteen negative results: the marginal value is in being more certain about what has been measured, not in measuring more things."));

children.push(H3("What a Sharpe ratio hides"));
children.push(P("Two inflations are invisible in the number itself: HOW MANY STRATEGIES WERE TRIED (the maximum of N draws is not the mean of one), and THE SHAPE OF THE RETURNS (negative skew and fat tails both flatter a Sharpe). sharpe_standard_error already handles sampling error, but it assumes i.i.d. GAUSSIAN returns and knows nothing about the trial count. PSR and DSR close both gaps."));
children.push(P("The Probabilistic Sharpe Ratio (Bailey & Lopez de Prado 2012) gives the probability the TRUE Sharpe exceeds a benchmark: PSR(SR*) = Z[(SR - SR*)*sqrt(T-1) / sqrt(1 - g3*SR + (g4-1)/4*SR^2)]. Note the sign on g3: NEGATIVE skew enlarges the denominator and shrinks the probability. That is the point - the short-volatility profile that earns steadily and loses catastrophically is precisely what a bare Sharpe rewards."));
children.push(P("The Deflated Sharpe Ratio (2014) is a PSR whose benchmark is not chosen but DERIVED FROM THE NUMBER OF TRIALS: SR*0 = sqrt(V[SRn]) * [(1-g)*Zinv(1 - 1/N) + g*Zinv(1 - 1/(N*e))], with g the Euler-Mascheroni constant. It answers the question a Sharpe cannot: is this better than the best thing noise would have handed me?"));
children.push(P([B("No new dependency. "), T("The normal CDF comes from math.erf; its inverse is Acklam's rational approximation with one Halley refinement, accurate to <5e-10 against published quantiles. This project has ZERO SciPy imports and keeps it that way - the same reason feature_drift carries a closed-form Kolmogorov distribution.")]));

children.push(H3("The statistic proving itself"));
children.push(P("N strategies with EXACTLY ZERO true skill, keep the best, then judge it:"));
children.push(table([1700, 2200, 2100, 2100, 1760], [
  ["N tried", "Best SR/yr", "PSR vs 0", "SR* (noise)", "DSR"],
  ["5", "0.21", "0.640", "0.037", "0.253"],
  ["20", "0.96", "0.952", "0.055", "0.564"],
  ["100", "1.36", "0.990", "0.099", "0.363"],
  ["500", "1.89", "0.999", "0.114", "0.557"],
]));
children.push(P([B("At N = 500 the best of five hundred WORTHLESS strategies posts an annualised Sharpe of 1.89 and a PSR of 0.999 "), T("- a textbook false discovery, and a number most backtests would ship. DSR says 0.557. It is not fooled because its threshold rises with N in step with the winner.")]));
children.push(P("(The N = 5 and N = 20 rows are corrected in Section 3.60: the driver reproduces the 100 and 500 rows and every figure below exactly, but not the first two rows as published. At N = 5 neither statistic is fooled; PSR is fooled from N = 20 on.)"));

children.push(H3("Applied to Section 3.40, it says something Section 3.40 did not"));
children.push(P("The reversal book is the one place in this project with both a return series and a COUNTABLE, NON-NEGOTIABLE trial count: run_reversal_costs.py swept exactly eight hold x tail configurations and the 5-day decile book is the one that got reported. That is textbook selection, and DSR prices it."));
children.push(table([4200, 2900, 2760], [
  ["Measure", "Gross", "Net at 25 bp"],
  ["Observed SR (per period)", "+0.0276", "-0.1094"],
  ["Skew", "-0.765", "-0.749"],
  ["Kurtosis (3 = Gaussian)", "13.14", "13.07"],
  ["PSR vs 0 (naive)", "0.6865", "0.0229"],
  ["SR* from 8 trials", "+0.0660", "+0.0660"],
  ["DEFLATED SHARPE", "0.2492", "0.0007"],
]));
children.push(P([B("The gross Sharpe is below the best-of-8-trials noise threshold "), T("- +0.0276 observed against +0.0660 expected from noise alone. Section 3.40 concluded that reversal dies on transaction costs. DSR says it FAILS MULTIPLE-TESTING DEFLATION BEFORE A SINGLE BASIS POINT OF COST IS APPLIED. That is a strictly stronger statement, and it was not available from the Sharpe, the breakeven, or the SPA test.")]));
children.push(P([B("Later note: the trial count here was understated, which strengthens this result. "), T("Each of the 8 configurations had 5 equally valid rebalance starting days, and the +0.0276 being deflated is the best of those five. A larger effective trial count can only raise SR*, so the book fails deflation by a wider margin than shown. The verdict is unchanged (Section 3.50).")]));
children.push(P("The shape statistics add a second layer Section 3.40 missed entirely: kurtosis 13.1 against 3 for a Gaussian, and skew -0.77. Section 3.40 described the gross Sharpe of 0.20 as 'thin'. It is worse than thin - it is thin, fat-tailed, negatively skewed, and below what chance would have produced from the same search."));

children.push(H3("Why this does not duplicate the SPA work"));
children.push(P("Section 3.23's SPA / Reality Check already corrects for selection bias across a model set, and the two are complementary rather than redundant on two counts. DSR uses the TRIAL COUNT AND THE DISPERSION OF THEIR SHARPES explicitly, where SPA works from the loss series. And DSR carries the SKEW AND KURTOSIS correction, which SPA does not - a strategy can clear SPA and still fail DSR if its returns are badly shaped or its peer trials were tightly clustered. On this data both reach the same verdict, which is itself worth knowing: two different corrections for the same disease agreeing is stronger evidence than either alone."));
children.push(P([B("No conclusion in this document changes. "), T("Section 3.40 already said the reversal signal is not tradeable; DSR says it was never distinguishable from the best of eight lucky draws either. probabilistic_sharpe_ratio, expected_max_sharpe and deflated_sharpe_ratio ship in strategy_metrics.py with 32 tests, and are available to any future result before it gets reported.")]));

children.push(H2("3.45 Directional Change (Tsang, AI for Finance, Ch. 6) - the fifteenth negative result, and a null that survived three controls"));
children.push(P("Tsang's AI for Finance (CRC Press, 2023) is a 126-page survey. Six of its seven chapters are commentary - AI-finance synergy, AlphaGo, Markowitz, blockchain, sentiment, 'finance as a hard science'. CHAPTER 6 IS THE ONLY ONE CONTAINING AN ALGORITHM, and it is a good one."));

children.push(H3("The representation"));
children.push(P("A time series records a price every fixed interval whether or not anything happened. DIRECTIONAL CHANGE inverts that: fix a threshold theta, and record nothing until the price reverses by theta from the last extreme. At that moment a DC event confirms and the trend flips. The market becomes alternating up- and down-trends, each split into a DC LEG (extreme to confirmation, a move of theta by construction) and an OVERSHOOT LEG (confirmation to next extreme, a move of whatever the market felt like - and therefore the only part carrying information)."));
children.push(P([B("This is the motivation behind Lopez de Prado's information-driven bars (AFML Ch. 2) with one decisive difference: "), T("tick bars need tick data, DC needs only a price path. It runs on the daily closes already in the panel. That is why this chapter was worth implementing and AFML Ch. 2 was not.")]));

children.push(H3("The lookahead trap the book waves away"));
children.push(P("Tsang notes DC events are 'recognized in hindsight' and argues this 'changes nothing'. For describing the past, true. For BUILDING FEATURES it is the most dangerous property of the representation, because the natural implementation is also the wrong one."));
children.push(P("The extreme starting a trend sits at t_ext, but nobody knows it is an extreme until t_confirm. Label the bars between them with the new trend's direction - which is exactly what a groupby over the event table gives you - and every one of those bars carries a fact from its own future. The model learns to buy the exact bottom."));
children.push(P("So directional_change.py ships TWO views rather than trusting the caller to remember: dc_events is the hindsight event table, safe for scaling laws and volatility measurement; dc_state is the per-bar causal view, and is the one that feeds a model. dc_state flips direction at the CONFIRMATION bar. The guarantee is tested, not asserted: test_dc_state_is_invariant_to_future_bars recomputes on truncated prefixes and requires the overlap to match at abs=0.0. Section 3.42 is this project's standing reminder of the cost - an open-interest signal at t = +2.88 became t = -0.27 once its publication lag was honoured."));
children.push(P("The module also reproduces the book's own Figure 6.1 exactly (trends of +7 and -22 as the text states), including the part the book does not say: the third trend is still running at the end of the data and must NOT be reported as though its end were known."));

children.push(H3("The 'striking discoveries' are theorems about Brownian motion"));
children.push(P("Chapter 6.5 presents two empirical laws from Olsen's FX work, 'across all major currency pairs', for which 'no explanation is available': (1) a trend ends on average at TWICE the threshold, i.e. mean(|OS|)/theta = 1; and (2) the overshoot leg takes TWICE AS LONG as the leg that confirmed it."));
children.push(P("There is an explanation. For driftless Brownian motion, the running maximum reached before a theta drawdown is EXPONENTIAL WITH MEAN THETA. Law 1 is that distribution's mean. Simulating a driftless random walk at increasing resolution:"));
children.push(table([1700, 1700, 1400, 1400, 1400, 1600, 1660], [
  ["steps per theta", "events", "DC/th", "OS/th", "OS/DC", "median OS/th", "t_OS/t_DC"],
  ["1", "63,201", "1.599", "1.622", "1.014", "0.848", "1.609"],
  ["5", "39,343", "1.118", "1.122", "1.004", "0.726", "1.940"],
  ["25", "10,914", "1.023", "1.026", "1.003", "0.712", "2.008"],
  ["predicted", "-", "1.000", "1.000", "1.000", "0.693", "2.000"],
]));
children.push(P([B("Both laws converge to their predicted values ON PURE NOISE. "), T("The median converges to ln 2 = 0.693 - and Tsang's own caveat, that 'the majority of trends ended far sooner than reaching twice the threshold', is precisely the exponential's mean-median gap. The book reads that asymmetry as an unexplained curiosity; it is the distribution's signature. A market obeying these laws is telling you it is a random walk. The interesting question is therefore not whether NSE obeys them, but whether it DEVIATES.")]));
children.push(P("The table also shows why the first row matters: at daily resolution a single session jumps well past theta, so the DC leg is NOT theta and OS/theta inflates with it. OS/DC sits at 1.00 at every resolution, which is why it is the statistic used below."));

children.push(H3("The drift trap, and then the crash trap"));
children.push(P("338 names, 2018-01-01 to 2024-06-30 - the same window as Sections 3.39, 3.40 and 3.46. Each name is compared against a SIGN-FLIPPED version of itself: its own log returns with randomised signs, so volatility clustering and fat tails are not merely matched but IDENTICAL, and only direction is destroyed."));
children.push(table([1300, 1700, 1600, 1500, 1400, 1300, 1160], [
  ["theta", "events/name", "OS/DC real", "demeaned", "flip null", "t raw", "t demeaned"],
  ["2%", "295.0", "1.143", "1.133", "1.002", "+10.56", "+9.45"],
  ["3%", "208.9", "1.145", "1.133", "1.006", "+10.35", "+8.71"],
  ["5%", "122.1", "1.133", "1.115", "1.006", "+9.48", "+7.26"],
  ["10%", "50.0", "1.175", "1.127", "1.013", "+9.11", "+4.47"],
]));
children.push(P([B("Two traps, and the first one nearly caught me. "), T("Both nulls are driftless, so a trending market shows a deviation for reasons unconnected to Directional Change. That is why scaling_law_test defaults to demean=True, and it is a large effect: on the 2022-2024 sub-window these names drifted at +16.7%/yr, the raw test read t = +6.22, and demeaning collapsed it to +0.15. Over the full window the drift is only +1.3%/yr, so demeaning barely moves the number - and a deviation of t ~ 4.5-9.4 survives at every threshold. That looks like a finding. It is a CRASH.")]));
children.push(table([2800, 1700, 1600, 1600, 1260], [
  ["period (theta = 5%)", "names", "real", "flip null", "t"],
  ["full 2018-2024", "338", "1.115", "1.022", "+5.95"],
  ["pre 2018-2019", "338", "1.053", "1.015", "+1.79"],
  ["2020 only", "317", "1.226", "1.005", "+7.50"],
  ["post 2021-2024", "308", "1.038", "1.002", "+2.17"],
]));
children.push(P([B("In the two non-COVID sub-periods the deviation is t = 1.79 and t = 2.17; in 2020 alone it is +7.50. "), T("The mechanism is plain once seen: in a crash every name trends hard in one direction for weeks, and long runs of same-sign returns are exactly what a sign flip destroys. The null is doing its job; there is simply no standing regularity for it to find.")]));
children.push(P([B("The fifteenth negative result, and the most instructive one, because the deviation survived three controls before failing the fourth. "), T("The iid-Gaussian and sign-flip nulls agree to within 0.02 at every threshold, so fat tails and volatility clustering genuinely were not the explanation. Demeaning handled the drift. What remained was one year. Section 3.39's lesson was that an underpowered test finds nothing; this one's is that a WELL-POWERED test against good nulls can still be measuring a single episode, and the only defence is to cut the sample and look. The TIME law needs none of this care: t_OS/t_DC matches the flip null at the two wider thresholds (t = +0.57 and -1.60) with no consistent sign.")]));
children.push(P([B("Both traps ship as code, not as narrative. "), T("DRIFTLESS_THEORY carries the Brownian limits (1.0, ln 2, 2.0), so every scaling statistic has its reference point attached. scaling_law_test(prices, theta, demean=True) runs the sign-flip null and returns a z per statistic, with DEMEAN DEFAULTING TO TRUE. test_drift_alone_manufactures_a_deviation_unless_demeaned reproduces the drift artefact on demand: a random walk plus +26%/yr, containing NO DIRECTIONAL STRUCTURE BY CONSTRUCTION, reports OS/DC = 1.510 against a null of 1.001 (z = +5.18); demeaned it gives 1.072 and z = +0.71. The sub-period split is a step of the driver rather than a note, because the crash trap has no closed form to guard against - the only defence is to cut the sample and look, every time.")]));

children.push(H3("What survives: Chapter 6.3"));
children.push(P("Chapter 6.3's two DC volatility measures - event frequency and mean trend magnitude - are claimed to be 'independent of the volatility measures under Time Series' and orthogonal to each other. Over 19,667 name-months at theta = 5%: DC event count vs realised vol rho = +0.53; mean trend magnitude vs realised vol rho = +0.39; and the two DC measures against each other rho = -0.11."));
children.push(P([B("'Independent' overstates it "), T("- a more volatile month does change direction more often. But neither is a restatement of realised volatility, and the two are close to orthogonal to each other exactly as claimed. CHAPTER 6.3 HOLDS UP. These are genuinely distinct measurements of a quantity this project already measures two other ways (Section 3.41's range estimators, and the realised volatility feeding HAR-RV).")]));

children.push(H3("And what that distinctness is worth"));
children.push(P("Chapter 6.4 promises that 'practitioners equipped with more variables will have a better chance to beat their competitors'. Fama-MacBeth rank IC of the causal dc_state features against 5-day forward excess returns, 488,096 rows, 334 names, 1,594 dates, Newey-West at the horizon:"));
children.push(table([3400, 2200, 1800, 2460], [
  ["feature", "mean IC", "t", "hit rate"],
  ["bars_in_trend", "+0.0253", "+4.24", "0.58"],
  ["retrace", "+0.0104", "+3.52", "0.56"],
  ["bars_since_confirm", "+0.0162", "+3.48", "0.57"],
  ["overshoot", "-0.0099", "-2.51", "0.46"],
  ["move_from_ext", "-0.0127", "-2.33", "0.47"],
  ["direction", "-0.0053", "-1.27", "0.49"],
  ["trailing 5-day return", "-0.0267", "-4.58", "0.43"],
]));
children.push(P([B("Five features clear |t| > 2, and the best - bars_in_trend, at t = +4.24 - is rho = +0.001 with the trailing return. "), T("It is genuinely NOT the reversal signal. On the shorter window the best feature had been move_from_ext at rho = +0.62 with the trailing return, an obvious reversal proxy; this one is not, which is a much more interesting claim. It is also a proxy for something else. bars_in_trend counts how long the current trend has run, and A QUIET STOCK TAKES LONGER TO TRAVEL THETA: rho(bars_in_trend, 20-day realised vol) = -0.593; the IC of realised volatility itself is -0.0453 (t -5.05); and the IC of bars_in_trend neutralised within volatility quintiles is +0.0044 (t +1.39).")]));
children.push(P([B("Low volatility is a documented cross-sectional effect, "), T("realised volatility scores it MORE STRONGLY than the DC feature proxying it, and ranking bars_in_trend within volatility quintiles removes 83% of the IC along with its significance. So the verdict is the same as on the short window, reached by a different route and with better evidence: whichever window you pick, DC's apparent signal turns out to be a KNOWN FACTOR IN EVENT-BASED CLOTHING - reversal on 2022-2024, the low-volatility effect on 2018-2024 - and measured more weakly than the factor itself.")]));

children.push(H2("3.46 Event-based backtesting (Hilpisch, AI in Finance, Ch. 11) - what Section 3.40's number never showed"));
children.push(P("Hilpisch's Artificial Intelligence in Finance (O'Reilly, 2020) is a serious book, and most of it is already here: Chapters 7-8's dense and recurrent networks are the existing model zoo, Chapter 9's RL was triaged out in Section 3.27 for the reasons given there, Chapter 3's portfolio theory is a different product, and Chapter 10's vectorized backtesting is Section 3.23. CHAPTER 11 IS THE GAP, and it is a real one."));

children.push(H3("The objection"));
children.push(P("Every backtest in this project is VECTORIZED: multiply a signal by the realised return from entry to horizon. Section 3.40 is literally fwd = log(px.shift(-hold) / px) - anchor to horizon, one step. Correct arithmetic, and BLIND TO THE PATH. Four of the five days of every hold are never looked at. That is not a theoretical complaint here: Section 3.44 measured this exact book at kurtosis 13.14, and fat tails are made of intra-period paths a close-to-close return cannot see."));

children.push(H3("What the path actually looked like"));
children.push(P("19,664 positions, 319 rebalances, 2018-01-01 to 2024-06-30 - the same window and the same selection rule as Section 3.40, reconstructed to carry every position's daily bars. The rebuild correlates 0.9871 with Section 3.40's stored return series, so it is the same book. Maximum adverse excursion needs no strategy assumption: it is simply how far underwater a position went before its horizon."));
children.push(table([5200, 4760], [
  ["Measure", "Value"],
  ["Mean MAE", "-5.59%"],
  ["Median MAE", "-3.70%"],
  ["10th percentile", "-12.94%"],
  ["1st percentile", "-29.77%"],
  ["Share past -5%", "38.4%"],
  ["Share past -10%", "15.3%"],
  ["Book's mean return per rebalance", "+0.1293%"],
]));
children.push(P([B("The book earns thirteen basis points per rebalance. "), T("The average position inside it goes 5.6% against itself on the way there, and one in seven goes past -10%. Section 3.40 shows none of this, because a vectorized backtest structurally cannot. This is the section's durable result: assumption-free, large, and it reframes what Section 3.40's thin positive number meant. A book is not only its endpoint returns - it is also what you have to sit through to collect them, and nothing in this project had ever measured that.")]));

children.push(H3("Do stops help?  A prediction, written before running it"));
children.push(P("Recorded in the driver's docstring beforehand: THEY SHOULD HURT. Section 3.39 identified this signal as short-term reversal, and stopping out of a reversal trade sells exactly when the mean-reversion is strongest."));
children.push(table([2600, 1900, 1500, 1500, 1400, 1060], [
  ["rule", "mean/reb", "ann. SR", "vs base", "t paired", "stopped"],
  ["none", "+0.1293%", "+0.292", "-", "-", "0%"],
  ["stop 3%", "+0.1421%", "+0.409", "+0.117", "+0.08", "57.4%"],
  ["stop 5%", "+0.1763%", "+0.466", "+0.174", "+0.33", "38.4%"],
  ["stop 8%", "+0.2649%", "+0.702", "+0.411", "+1.23", "21.7%"],
  ["stop 10%", "+0.2232%", "+0.588", "+0.297", "+1.06", "15.3%"],
  ["trail 3%", "+0.0168%", "+0.071", "-0.221", "-0.67", "91.1%"],
  ["trail 5%", "+0.1301%", "+0.426", "+0.134", "+0.00", "68.4%"],
  ["stop 5% + tp 5%", "-0.0550%", "-0.245", "-0.537", "-1.20", "35.3%"],
]));
children.push(P([B("The prediction was wrong in sign, and the opposite is not established either. "), T("The Sharpe column invites a story - stop 8% 'improves' the book by +0.41 - that the paired difference series does not support: NOT ONE RULE REACHES |t| = 2, and the best is t = +1.23. Because the rules share dates and selection, the paired difference isolates the rule's effect; comparing two Sharpe ratios by eye does not.")]));
children.push(P("This is also an 8-configuration sweep with the best one being quoted, which is precisely what Section 3.44 built the instrument for. Applying it here rather than to someone else's result: observed SR per period +0.0989, SR* from 8 trials +0.0620, naive PSR vs 0 of 0.9673, and a DEFLATED SHARPE of 0.7538. It clears the noise floor, unlike Section 3.40's book, but 0.75 is not a result. Recorded as predicted-and-refuted rather than quietly dropped - the pre-registration discipline is worthless if the prediction only gets reported when it lands."));
children.push(P([B("What a stop demonstrably does deliver is the distributional change it promises: "), T("skew moves from +0.234 to +1.001 and kurtosis from 9.54 to 7.54. Truncating the left tail is what a stop is for, and it does it. That is a distributional fact, not a risk-adjusted return, and the two are routinely conflated. (A note on Section 3.44: its skew of -0.781 is roughly half a CONVENTION effect - the same series in simple returns is -0.269. Log returns are right for compounding and simple returns for 'what filled at what price', so both are correct for what they measure. Section 3.44's conclusion is unaffected: its SR* threshold comes from the trial Sharpes' dispersion and does not use skew at all.)")]));

children.push(H3("Where a stop-loss backtest lies"));
children.push(P("Getting this right is most of the module. A stop is NOT A GUARANTEED FILL - if the bar opens through the level you get the open, not the stop."));
children.push(table([2200, 2600, 2900, 2360], [
  ["stop", "honest (gap fill)", "assumes fill at stop", "overstated by"],
  ["3%", "+0.1421%", "+0.4141%", "+0.2721%"],
  ["5%", "+0.1763%", "+0.4506%", "+0.2744%"],
  ["8%", "+0.2649%", "+0.3923%", "+0.1273%"],
]));
children.push(P([B("At a 5% stop the naive assumption is worth +13.8% a year of pure fiction, "), T("and on the 2022-2024 sub-window it flips the sign of the result outright. With kurtosis 13, gapping through a stop is not an edge case - it is the case. Two more, both defaults in event_backtest.py: INTRABAR ORDERING (if one bar's low hits the stop and its high hits the target, daily data cannot say which came first; adverse_first=True assumes the stop, because the alternative is free money) and TRAILING RATCHETS (raising the trail on bar i's high and then testing bar i's low against the new level assumes the high came first; the trail is computed from bars strictly before i, and one test pins ~26% of fictional profit on a single constructed bar).")]));

children.push(H3("Later check: all five rebalance starting days"));
children.push(P("Section 3.50 found that a 5-day book rebalanced every 5 sessions has five equally valid starting days, and that Section 3.40's, which this driver copies, is the best of them. So the whole driver was re-run on each (--phase 0..4):"));
children.push(table([2900, 1420, 1410, 1410, 1410, 1410], [
  ["", "phase 0 (above)", "1", "2", "3", "4"],
  ["Book's mean per rebalance", "+0.1293%", "+0.0286%", "-0.0756%", "-0.0450%", "+0.0956%"],
  ["Mean MAE", "-5.59%", "-5.62%", "-5.62%", "-5.64%", "-5.65%"],
  ["Share past -10%", "15.3%", "15.6%", "15.4%", "15.8%", "15.3%"],
  ["stop 8%, t paired", "+1.23", "+0.57", "-0.27", "-0.65", "-0.77"],
  ["Best rule of the sweep", "stop 8%", "trail 5%", "trail 3%", "stop 5%", "stop 10%"],
  ["Its deflated Sharpe", "0.75", "0.65", "0.41", "0.08", "0.24"],
  ["stop 5% + tp 5%, t paired", "-1.20", "-0.57", "-0.67", "-1.23", "-1.79"],
  ["Gap-fill overstatement, 5% stop", "+0.274%", "+0.257%", "+0.254%", "+0.286%", "+0.294%"],
]));
children.push(P([B("The two durable results survive unchanged. "), T("The drawdown inside the hold (mean MAE -5.6%, about one position in seven past -10%) and the gap-fill overstatement (+0.25% to +0.29% per rebalance at a 5% stop) barely move between starting days. Both are properties of the positions and of how stops fill, not of which days the book trades on.")]));
children.push(P([B("The stop-loss point estimate does not survive. "), T("Stop 8%'s improvement was a phase-0 result: across the five starting days its paired t runs from +1.23 to -0.77, and the average change in Sharpe is +0.008. The best rule is a different one on every starting day, and its deflated Sharpe falls from 0.75 to as low as 0.08. So the honest reading of 'do stops help?' is now no measurable effect in either direction. The original prediction (stops should hurt) is not supported either. The one rule with the same sign on every starting day is the TAKE-PROFIT: stop 5% + tp 5% is negative on all five (t -0.57 to -1.79). Capping a reversal trade's upside cuts off the mean reversion it is paid for. No single starting day clears |t| = 2, and the five share most of their positions' days, so this is a consistent sign, not a finding.")]));
children.push(P("Section 3.44's kurtosis of 13.14 is also the phase-0 figure. In simple returns phase 0 gives 9.54 and the other four give 3.7-5.8. That is still fat-tailed, but well below 13, and the gap-fill result does not depend on it."));
children.push(H3("The rest of the book"));
children.push(P("Chapter 11 was the gap. The full triage, since a partial reading presented as a complete one is the failure mode Section 3.27 exists to avoid:"));
children.push(table([1200, 4200, 4560], [
  ["Ch.", "Topic", "Status here"],
  ["1-2", "AI basics; superintelligence", "Nothing implementable - Ch. 2 is Bostrom-style philosophy"],
  ["3", "Normative finance: EUT, mean-variance, CAPM, APT", "A different product, and moot without a directional edge to allocate"],
  ["4", "Debunking central assumptions (normality, linearity)", "True, already known, already acted on - see below"],
  ["5", "ML fundamentals: capacity, bias/variance, CV", "Walk-forward with purging and embargo is stricter than plain CV"],
  ["6", "AI-first finance / efficient markets", "The book reaches this project's conclusion - see below"],
  ["7", "Dense nets: normalization, dropout, bagging, optimizers", "All present except bagging - see below"],
  ["8", "Recurrent neural networks", "The existing model zoo (LSTM, GRU, Transformer, TCN, N-BEATS)"],
  ["9", "Reinforcement learning (DQL/FQL, finance gym)", "Triaged out in Section 3.27; RL optimises a directional policy, which Section 3.16 says has no edge to find"],
  ["10", "Vectorized backtesting", "Section 3.23"],
  ["11", "Risk management, event-based backtesting", "IMPLEMENTED - this section"],
  ["12", "Execution and deployment (Oanda)", "Broker integration for FX; this is an NSE project and does not place orders"],
  ["13-14", "AI-based competition; financial singularity", "Outlook, not method"],
]));
children.push(P([B("Chapter 4 "), T("argues that normally distributed returns and linear relationships are false, via D'Agostino-Pearson skew/kurtosis tests and Q-Q plots. Both claims are correct and neither is news here: Section 3.44 measures the skew and kurtosis directly and feeds them into PSR, Section 3.25 does not merely ASSUME non-normality but TESTS IT WHERE IT HAS CONSEQUENCES - five VaR estimators, Gaussian among them, scored by Kupiec and Christoffersen - and the entire conformal layer is distribution-free precisely because the assumption fails. Adding a formal normality test would confirm a premise the codebase is already built on, and would mean hand-rolling D'Agostino to preserve the project's zero-SciPy discipline.")]));
children.push(P([B("Chapter 6 "), T("is the book's central empirical claim, and it is worth recording what it actually concludes. Hilpisch lags log returns, fits OLS and an MLPRegressor, and reports in-sample direction accuracy 'significantly above 50% throughout and above 60% in a few cases'. He then states plainly that this was fit and predicted on the same rows, repeats it with an 80/20 split, and finds the neural network 'much worse when compared to the in-sample numbers and SIMILAR TO THE OLS REGRESSION RESULTS' - that is, ~50%.")]));
children.push(P([B("So a book explicitly looking for AI-discoverable inefficiency in price history reports the same answer this project reached across fifteen negative results, "), T("on a single 80/20 split where this one uses walk-forward validation, purging, embargo, SPA/Reality Check and a deflated Sharpe. Independent corroboration from a source motivated to find the opposite is worth more than another confirming experiment would be.")]));
children.push(P([B("Chapter 7's bagging "), T("is the one genuine absence: this project stacks tree learners and takes a cross-family median (meta_ensemble.py), but never bootstrap-aggregates. It is deliberately left. Bagging reduces VARIANCE, not bias - averaging many draws of a predictor with no edge yields a lower-variance predictor with no edge - and epistemic.py already asks the neighbouring question ('how much would this forecast change if the model had been fitted differently?') by Monte-Carlo dropout, recorded as the eighth negative result in Section 3.34. Building it would add a sixteenth trial against fifteen nulls with a strongly predictable outcome, which is the behaviour Section 3.44's deflation exists to penalise.")]));

children.push(H3("The window, and an error worth recording"));
children.push(P([B("The first run of this driver defaulted to 2022-01-01 rather than Section 3.40's 2018-01-01, "), T("copied without thinking from the Section 3.45 driver. On that sub-window the same book posts an annualised Sharpe of 1.01 against 0.24 over the full period, stops appeared to HURT exactly as predicted, and every conclusion would have been a sample-period artefact that happened to confirm the hypothesis. It was caught by reconciling the rebuild against Section 3.40's stored CSV, which is now STEP 0 of the driver and prints the correlation before anything else. A driver that reconstructs another section's result should prove it reconstructed it. event_backtest.py ships with 24 tests, most of which fail toward optimism rather than crashing.")]));

children.push(H2("3.47 Price precision (Buzzelli, Data Quality Engineering, Ch. 3) - the dimension that was missing, and a falsification of Section 3.39"));
children.push(P("Buzzelli's Data Quality Engineering in Financial Services (O'Reilly) borrows its frame from manufacturing: a bottling plant does not inspect water by looking at it, it writes a SPECIFICATION WITH TOLERANCES for each quality dimension and alarms on breach. Chapters 3-5 name eight such dimensions for data. Chapters 7-10 are governance - councils, operating models, maturity assessments - which is organisational process, not technique, and not applicable to a single-developer research codebase."));
children.push(P("The eight dimensions against what data_quality.py already checked: COMPLETENESS (covered - nan_price_frac, min_rows_required, max_gap_days, zero_volume_frac), TIMELINESS (covered - last_bar_age_days), ACCURACY (covered - bad_ohlc_frac, nonpositive_prices, recent_max_jump), CONFORMITY (covered - numeric coercion, fx_conversion), COLLECTION (covered - duplicate_timestamps, sessions), COHESION (partly - OHLC internal consistency), PRECISION (NOTHING), CONGRUENCE (nothing - see the end)."));
children.push(P([B("Seven of eight were already covered, "), T("which is a reasonable score for a gate written without the framework. PRECISION was the genuine gap, and it is the one that fails quietly: a coarse price grid produces no missing value, no bad OHLC row and no gap. It produces a return series that looks perfectly well-formed and is partly made of the tick.")]));

children.push(H3("Measuring it in the units that matter"));
children.push(P("Not the tick in rupees, and not the tick as a fraction of price. NSE quotes almost everything on a Rs 0.05 grid, which is 0.002% of a Rs 3,000 share and 2% of a Rs 2.40 one - but a 2% grid is irrelevant to a stock that moves 8% a day and crippling to one that moves 3%. The quantity that separates them is the tick as a share of ONE DAILY STANDARD DEVIATION. Over the 400-name panel, 2018-01-01 to 2024-06-30: 50th percentile 0.6%, 90th 3.5%, 99th 25.7%, max 108.7%."));
children.push(P("For the typical name this is a non-issue and the check will never fire. 11 of 335 names exceed 10%, and they are all distressed penny stocks:"));
children.push(table([2100, 1700, 1700, 2100, 2360], [
  ["symbol", "price", "tick (bp)", "grid share", "repeated returns"],
  ["LIQUIDBEES", "Rs 1,000", "0.1", "108.7%", "99.4%"],
  ["MERCATOR", "Rs 2.4", "208", "41.7%", "45.7%"],
  ["UNITECH", "Rs 3.0", "164", "30.9%", "46.9%"],
  ["RCOM", "Rs 3.0", "167", "28.9%", "49.5%"],
  ["KWALITY", "Rs 6.2", "81", "19.6%", "36.2%"],
]));
children.push(P([B("A 'repeated return' is a day whose log return EXACTLY equals another day's "), T("- the fingerprint of a grid rather than a market. For UNITECH and RCOM, roughly HALF of all daily returns are exact duplicates; a volatility model fitted to that is measuring the tick. LIQUIDBEES at 99.4% is the money-market ETF that tools/panel.py's instrument-type audit independently flagged for near-zero volatility. Two unrelated measurements - a volatility floor and a precision ceiling - pointing at the same instrument is a good sign that both are working.")]));
children.push(P([B("COARSE_PRICE_GRID now ships in data_quality.py, "), T("at INFO above 5% and WARN above 15%.
