It deliberately does NOT force abstention: a penny stock is still forecastable, just noisily, and a data-quality check that silently takes the app offline is a behaviour change wearing a diagnostic's clothes. test_a_coarse_grid_alone_does_not_force_abstention pins that.")]));

children.push(H3("The question worth asking: is Section 3.39's signal made of the grid?"));
children.push(P("This is where a data-quality chapter turns into a research result. Roll (1984) showed that BID-ASK BOUNCE INDUCES NEGATIVE SERIAL CORRELATION in transaction prices, and price discreteness compounds it. Negative short-horizon autocorrelation is precisely the shape of the short-term reversal signal that Section 3.39 identified as this project's one positive result. If that signal lived in the coarse-grid names, it would be microstructure rather than predictability - and it would explain mechanically why Section 3.40 found it costs more to trade than it earns. So the panel is split into precision quintiles and the reversal IC measured inside each:"));
children.push(table([1700, 1300, 1700, 1900, 1700, 1800, 1260], [
  ["quintile", "names", "grid share", "median price", "lag-1 AC", "reversal IC", "t"],
  ["1 (finest)", "67", "0.1%", "Rs 1,697", "+0.0072", "-0.0267", "-2.66"],
  ["2", "67", "0.3%", "Rs 637", "+0.0214", "-0.0323", "-3.44"],
  ["3", "67", "0.6%", "Rs 290", "+0.0285", "-0.0363", "-3.68"],
  ["4", "67", "1.4%", "Rs 119", "+0.0327", "-0.0486", "-4.75"],
  ["5 (coarsest)", "67", "3.5%", "Rs 43", "+0.0632", "-0.0439", "-3.84"],
]));
children.push(P([B("Two things, and both cut against the microstructure explanation. "), T("First, lag-1 autocorrelation goes the WRONG WAY FOR ROLL: it RISES from +0.0072 on the finest grid to +0.0632 on the coarsest, where bid-ask bounce predicts it should fall and turn negative. There is a clean reason - NSE's official close is a CALL-AUCTION PRICE (volume-weighted over the last half hour), not a random buy or sell print, so the bounce Roll's model assumes is largely averaged away before the close is struck. Roll's mechanism is about TRANSACTION prices; this panel is built from auction prices.")]));
children.push(P([B("Second, reversal is present at every precision level, "), T("including the finest quintile at a median price of Rs 1,697 where the tick is one part in ten thousand of a daily move (IC -0.0267, t -2.66). There is a mild tilt toward the coarser names, but it is not monotone - quintile 5 is weaker than quintile 4 - and the quintiles differ in far more than precision, since cheap stocks are also smaller and more volatile.")]));
children.push(P([B("Section 3.39's result survives a falsification attempt it had not previously faced. "), T("That is the honest value of this section: not a new signal, but one fewer way for an existing one to be wrong. The project has fifteen negative results; the one positive result deserves the same adversarial treatment, and this is the first time its MEASUREMENT SUBSTRATE rather than its statistics was attacked.")]));

children.push(H3("Congruence, considered and declined"));
children.push(P([B("The eighth uncovered dimension is CONGRUENCE "), T("- do independent sources agree? The hook exists: get_data.py reads both yfinance and nselib/bhavcopy, and safe_fetch falls back between them. Section 3.39 validated the two against each other once, at return correlation 1.00000 across 24/24 names. A CONTINUOUS check is not built, and the reason is architectural rather than theoretical: comparing sources on every fetch means fetching both every time, doubling network cost and latency for a check that has so far always passed. The one-off validation is recorded, and the day the fetch layer maintains two live sources anyway, the check becomes free and worth adding. Noted rather than silently skipped.")]));
children.push(H2("3.48 The identifier is not the ticker (Khraisha, Financial Data Engineering, Ch. 3) - a hole in the survivorship-free panel"));
children.push(P("Khraisha's Financial Data Engineering is 507 pages of INFRASTRUCTURE: ingestion protocols, storage models, data lakes, message brokers, Postgres, Mage, Netflix Conductor, microservice workflows. Almost all of it describes an enterprise data platform, and this project is a single-user app with a research harness - building a data lake here would be a different product, not an improvement. Chapter 5's nine data-quality dimensions substantially duplicate Section 3.47's Buzzelli mapping, and Chapter 10's observability layer is covered by prediction_log.py and feature_drift.py. CHAPTER 3 IS THE EXCEPTION, and it found something real."));

children.push(H3("The property that matters"));
children.push(P("Among the properties Khraisha requires of a financial identifier, two are decisive. PERMANENCE: identifiers and their associated data must be durable over time. IMMUTABILITY: 'an identifier associated with a specific entity should neither change nor be reassigned to another entity. If the identified entity ceases to exist, its identifier should never be reused.'"));
children.push(P([B("Ticker symbols satisfy neither, "), T("and Section 3.39's survivorship-free panel is keyed on the NSE symbol. So I went looking for symbol REUSE - two companies spliced into one price series, which would be the mirror image of the survivorship bias Section 3.39 exists to remove. That is not what is there. What is there is worse, in the sense that it is structural rather than incidental.")]));

children.push(H3("NSE's key is (SYMBOL, SERIES), and the panel uses half of it"));
children.push(P("bhavcopy.read_legacy keeps SERIES == 'EQ'. That is correct and deliberate - the file also carries government securities (GS), treasury bills (TB) and the trade-for-trade segments (BE, BZ), none of which belong in an equity cross-section, and the docstring said so. What the docstring did NOT say is the CONSEQUENCE. A company is not fixed in one series. NSE moves it to 'BE' under the Additional Surveillance Measure and back to 'EQ' when released. A live, trading company therefore disappears from the panel for the length of its surveillance period and reappears afterwards."));
children.push(P("Verified against the raw NSE archive for 2018-08-01 rather than inferred: RELIANCE sits in series EQ and is in the panel; RAIN and BOMDYEING sit in series BE and are not. Both vanish for 78 sessions and return. I found them because two large caps shared an IDENTICAL gap start, length and coverage - which looked like a download hole. It is not: the local cache is 100% complete for every business day 2018-2024, and those names are genuinely absent from the EQ rows."));

children.push(H3("How big is it"));
children.push(P("Name-days missing INSIDE each name's own listed span: 1.33%. Names with any interior gap: 58 of 339. Names losing more than 10% of their own history: 21."));
children.push(table([2600, 1900, 1900, 3560], [
  ["symbol", "missing", "of span", "share of its own history"],
  ["SHILPI", "72", "131", "55%"],
  ["ARCOTECH", "367", "730", "50%"],
  ["MERCATOR", "385", "1,084", "36%"],
  ["SINTEX", "319", "1,041", "31%"],
  ["RCOM", "247", "1,084", "23%"],
  ["UNITECH", "105", "462", "23%"],
  ["ABAN", "314", "1,507", "21%"],
  ["KWALITY", "154", "777", "20%"],
]));
children.push(P([B("Small in aggregate, concentrated and non-random in distribution. "), T("The exchange applies surveillance precisely when a name is volatile, illiquid or suspected of manipulation - so the panel is missing these names exactly during their most unusual periods. It is a CONDITIONAL survivorship hole inside a panel built to be survivorship-free. Note the overlap: MERCATOR, UNITECH, RCOM and KWALITY appear here, in Section 3.47's coarse-price-grid list, and in tools/panel.py's flat-instrument audit. Three unrelated data-quality lenses converging on the same small cluster is evidence all three are measuring something real.")]));

children.push(H3("The rest of the book"));
children.push(P("Chapter 3 was the whole of it. The triage, verified against the code rather than read off the contents page:"));
children.push(table([900, 3900, 5060], [
  ["Ch.", "Topic", "Status here"],
  ["1", "The financial-data-engineer role, skills", "Career description, not method"],
  ["2", "Data ecosystem: sources, types, benchmark datasets (CRSP, Compustat, TAQ, IBES)", "A catalogue of feeds this project cannot license - the same wall Section 3.33 hit"],
  ["3", "Identifier permanence and immutability", "IMPLEMENTED - this section"],
  ["4", "Financial NER and entity resolution", "Needed to join name-keyed against ticker-keyed data; this project joins nothing that way"],
  ["5", "Nine data-quality dimensions; integrity; security", "Mostly duplicates Section 3.47's Buzzelli mapping - see below"],
  ["6", "Lifecycle: open source vs commercial, cloud vs on-prem, multi-cloud", "Procurement and deployment architecture"],
  ["7-9", "Ingestion protocols, storage models, transformation and delivery", "An enterprise data platform; building one here would be a different product"],
  ["10", "Monitoring: metrics, events, logs, traces, observability", "Covered - data_quality.py, feature_drift.py, prediction_log.py, run_manifest"],
  ["11", "ETL, stream, microservice and ML workflows", "Workflow orchestration for a multi-service platform"],
  ["12", "Hands-on: PostgreSQL, Mage, Netflix Conductor, OpenFIGI", "Infrastructure tutorials"],
]));
children.push(P([B("Chapter 5's dimension list adds one category Buzzelli's eight did not: DATA BIASES. "), T("Khraisha names five, and they are the reason to check rather than assume. SELF-SELECTION (voluntary reporting) is not applicable - exchange bhavcopy is mandatory. SURVIVORSHIP was closed by Section 3.39 at a 26.8% gap, and this section found the second-order version. BACKFILLING was measured: 1 name in 339 first traded after the window opened, and the liquidity screen selects on a PRE-WINDOW lookback, so a later-listed name is never backfilled in. LOOK-AHEAD is handled by Section 3.7's purging/embargo, adjust_prices(as_of=) and Section 3.42's publication lag. DATA SNOOPING is handled by Section 3.16's SPA / Reality Check and Section 3.44's deflated Sharpe.")]));
children.push(P([B("Khraisha's headline look-ahead example is a fundamentals publication lag "), T("- an annual report for 2019 released in March 2020 must not be visible on 2019-12-31. That is the same error class that cost Section 3.42 a signal, so it was worth checking rather than assuming. fundamentals.py already carries PUBLICATION_LAG_DAYS = 45 and ANNUAL_PUBLICATION_LAG_DAYS = 60, computes available_from(period_end), and joins with a BACKWARD AS-OF MERGE on the lagged date. Better than that: as_of_join REFUSES a frame that is not marked point-in-time rather than warning about it - the same make-the-safe-thing-mandatory pattern as scaling_law_test(demean=True) and corwin_schultz(gap_adjust=True). Nothing to add.")]));
children.push(P([B("Chapter 5's integrity principles "), T("- standards, backups, archiving, aggregation, lineage, catalogs, ownership, contracts, reconciliation - are organisational except two. LINEAGE in Khraisha's sense is a regulatory AUDIT TRAIL (OATS, CAT, order-level traceability for spoofing investigations); this project places no orders, and the reproducibility form of lineage is run_manifest plus get_data.py's attrs['price_adjustment']. RECONCILIATION against an independent source is the congruence dimension Section 3.47 declined, for the same architectural reason: it means fetching both sources on every call.")]));
children.push(H3("The filter stays, and the reason is about trading"));
children.push(P([B("The fix is NOT to include BE. "), T("A trade-for-trade line settles with no intraday netting and full margin - you cannot buy and sell it the same day. A backtest that traded a BE name as though it were a normal EQ line would be assuming an execution that was not available, which is the same class of error as Section 3.40's cost assumptions and Section 3.46's gap-fill. So the exclusion is right. SILENTLY excluding was not.")]));
children.push(P("Two things changed: bhavcopy.read_legacy's docstring now states the consequence, the verification and the measured size - not just the rule; and tools/panel.py reports interior gaps alongside its ETF and precision audits, so the hole is visible on the research path, which never calls the app's quality gate. No published figure moves: at 1.33% of name-days, and with the affected names absent rather than wrong, nothing in Sections 3.39-3.47 is recomputed. What changes is that the next person reading a 55% coverage figure for SHILPI will know why."));
children.push(P([B("The general lesson is Khraisha's. "), T("A compound key collapsed to one of its components will lose rows silently, and the loss will correlate with whatever the dropped component encodes - here, distress. The project had already paid for the SURVIVORSHIP version of this mistake in Section 3.39; this is the same mistake one level down.")]));
children.push(H2("3.49 Risk-neutral is not physical (Hilpisch, Financial Theory with Python, Ch. 5) - a calibration error in shipped code"));
children.push(P("Hilpisch's Financial Theory with Python is a discrete-state mathematical finance textbook: two-state and three-state economies, replication, Arrow-Debreu securities, martingale pricing, both Fundamental Theorems of Asset Pricing, utility maximisation and equilibrium, then Black-Scholes-Merton, Merton jump-diffusion, binomial trees and Longstaff-Schwartz. Nearly all of it prices DERIVATIVES or allocates a PORTFOLIO, and this project does neither - it has no option chain, and portfolio optimisation has been declined three times already for the same reason: allocating capital needs a directional edge, and Sections 3.40 and 3.44 say there isn't one. But Chapter 5's central distinction - that option-implied quantities live under the RISK-NEUTRAL measure while realised quantities live under the PHYSICAL one - lands on something this project ships."));

children.push(H3("The bug"));
children.push(P("Section 3.20 tilts the HAR-RV cone by an India VIX signal: F = (VIX-implied market daily vol) / (NIFTY recently-realised daily vol); w = R-squared of the stock against NIFTY; m = clip(1 + w*(F - 1), [0.85, 1.30]). Section 3.20 describes F > 1 as 'the market prices in MORE forward vol than recently realised (widen the cone)'. That reading ASSUMES F = 1 IS NEUTRAL - that implied and realised are two estimates of the same thing."));
children.push(P("They are not. Implied volatility is a risk-neutral expectation and carries the VARIANCE RISK PREMIUM: the compensation sellers of options demand for bearing variance risk. It sits above realised volatility structurally, not occasionally. Measured on 2,428 days of India VIX against NIFTY:"));
children.push(table([4200, 5760], [
  ["F = implied / realised", "value"],
  ["5th percentile", "0.859"],
  ["median", "1.270"],
  ["95th percentile", "1.814"],
  ["share of days with F > 1", "83.7%"],
]));
children.push(P([B("So on a typical day F is about 1.27 and the multiplier is 1 + w*(0.27) - between 1.05 and 1.16 for realistic linkage. "), T("Averaged over the whole history at w = 0.4, the mean multiplier was 1.1097. That is a CONSTANT 11% WIDENING OF THE CONE, presented as a forward-looking signal. It is not information about what the market expects; it is the price of a measure change, and it would have been applied on 84% of days regardless of conditions.")]));

children.push(H3("Why the original A/B did not catch it"));
children.push(P([B("Section 3.20 records that the feature ships OFF after an A/B showed it 'coverage-neutral on calm data'. "), T("That result is EXACTLY WHAT A CONSTANT WIDENING LOOKS LIKE when coverage is already at or above target - the extra width eats headroom that was already there. The A/B compared 'on' against 'off' and measured coverage; it could not distinguish forward-looking information from a multiplier of 1.11, because on calm data both produce the same slightly-wider, slightly-over-covering cone. A null result is not evidence that a component is correct. It is evidence that the test could not tell the difference - which is a different statement, and the one worth recording.")]));

children.push(H3("The fix"));
children.push(P("Divide F by its own TRAILING MEDIAN, so the factor responds to how the premium compares with its normal level rather than to the premium itself. The baseline uses 504 trailing sessions with a 252-session minimum, computed strictly as-of, so it stays leak-free in a walk-forward backtest for the same reason every other lookup in the module does. Re-measured on the real series with a strictly trailing baseline:"));
children.push(table([3200, 2300, 2100, 2360], [
  ["", "median F", "share > 1", "mean multiplier (w = 0.4)"],
  ["raw (before)", "1.270", "83.7%", "1.1097"],
  ["centred (after)", "0.996", "49.5%", "1.0044"],
]));
children.push(P([B("The factor is now genuinely two-sided "), T("- it widens and tightens about equally often - and its AVERAGE effect on the cone is +0.4% rather than +11%, while still responding to a VIX that is high or low FOR ITSELF. With too little history to form a baseline the factor returns a NO-OP rather than the uncentred ratio; falling back to the raw value would reintroduce the premium silently, and a miscalibrated signal is worse than no signal.")]));
children.push(P([B("The regression test is deliberately not 'the multiplier equals 1'. "), T("Today's realised vol still differs from its median and that difference is real signal. It asserts the property the centring actually guarantees: the same market path at VIX 8, 15 and 60 must give the same factor, because the level of the premium is a property of the measure change, not of the forecast. The raw ratio varies by more than 5x across those levels; the centred one by less than 0.02.")]));

children.push(H3("What this changes"));
children.push(P([B("Nothing that ships. "), T("iv_adjust remains OFF BY DEFAULT, so no forecast anyone has seen was affected - the feature was already disabled on the strength of an A/B that, it turns out, was measuring the wrong thing. What changes is that the opt-in path is now calibrated, and if the A/B is ever re-run it will compare against a signal rather than against a constant. This is the FIRST thing a book has found that was an outright error in shipped code, rather than a technique to add or a null to record. It is also the smallest: one division, once the distinction is seen. Chapter 5 spends thirty pages on the measure change and this project had carefully implemented a ratio across it without noticing - which is a fair summary of why the theory chapters are worth reading even when the pricing ones are not.")]));
children.push(H2("3.50 Residual reversal (Tatsat et al., Blueprints, Ch. 7) - and a rebalance-phase artefact in Section 3.40"));
children.push(P("Machine Learning and Data Science Blueprints for Finance is a book of worked case studies, most outside this project (fraud, loan default, robo-advisors, yield curves, derivative pricing, chatbots) or declined before (RL trading, hierarchical risk parity, NLP sentiment). Its stock-prediction case study builds features cleanly but concludes that simple models are promising by comparing models against each other, with no random-walk baseline. Its bitcoin case study (Ch. 6, reused in Ch. 7) is a cautionary one: the target is TODAY's 10-versus-60-day moving-average crossover rather than any future move, one feature (MA21, in fact a 10-day average) is the crossover's own input, and the split is a shuffled train_test_split - it measures how well a classifier recovers a formula from its ingredients. Its pairs-trading study picks pairs by clustering over the whole sample, which is look-ahead. Chapter 7's PCA is the useful part: Section 3.39's reversal signal ranks on the RAW trailing 5-day return, part of which is a stock moving with common factors and snapping back with them. Removing the top principal components first asks whether the project's one positive result is idiosyncratic or a factor bouncing back. Pre-specified: PCA on a trailing 252-day window, k in {1, 3, 5}, decile long-short, 5-day hold, 2018-2024, stopping before the reserved final test period."));
children.push(H3("The reconciliation failed first, and found something"));
children.push(P("Rebuilding Section 3.40's raw book to check the driver gave a mean of -0.12% per rebalance against Section 3.40's +0.105% (correlation 0.256). The cause was not a bug: the driver's first rebalance landed two sessions later. A 5-day book rebalanced every 5 sessions has FIVE equally valid starting days, and the result depends on which one you pick:"));
children.push(table([2600, 2600, 2300, 2360], [
  ["Rebalance starting day", "Mean gross per rebalance", "Annual Sharpe", "t"],
  ["0 (Section 3.40's)", "+0.0873%", "+0.196", "+0.49"],
  ["1", "+0.0164%", "+0.041", "+0.10"],
  ["2", "-0.1004%", "-0.268", "-0.67"],
  ["3", "-0.0531%", "-0.145", "-0.36"],
  ["4", "+0.0704%", "+0.184", "+0.46"],
]));
children.push(P([B("Section 3.40 published the best of the five, not by choice "), T("- its loop started on row 5. Averaged over all five, gross is +0.004% and no starting day reaches |t| = 1. So the raw reversal book is indistinguishable from zero BEFORE costs, not just after them, which strengthens Sections 3.40 and 3.44. Section 3.39's IC is unaffected because it is computed every day. The driver now computes signals every day and averages the book over all five starting days; re-reconciled, it matches Section 3.40 at correlation 0.967.")]));
children.push(H3("The result"));
children.push(table([1900, 1300, 1100, 1900, 1900, 1860], [
  ["Signal", "Rank IC", "t", "Gross/rebalance (5-day avg)", "Range across starting days", "Breakeven"],
  ["raw", "-0.0266", "-4.16", "+0.025%", "-0.12% .. +0.15%", "1.4 bp"],
  ["residual k=1", "-0.0315", "-5.97", "+0.127%", "-0.03% .. +0.27%", "7.3 bp"],
  ["residual k=3", "-0.0306", "-6.80", "+0.191%", "+0.04% .. +0.29%", "11.0 bp"],
  ["residual k=5", "-0.0335", "-8.08", "+0.263%", "+0.17% .. +0.33%", "15.1 bp"],
]));
children.push(P([B("Most of the reversal that survives is the stock's own, not a factor's. "), T("Removing common factors nearly doubles the t-statistic (-4.16 to -8.08), and the k = 5 book is positive on every starting day. All three pre-specified k values beat the raw signal, and turnover is unchanged at 3.47, so the improvement flows straight into breakeven: 1.4 bp to 15.1 bp (re-run after the renamed-company fix, Section 3.75). It is still not tradeable - 15.1 bp is below STT's 20 bp round trip, and only 39% of a liquid NSE universe is shortable. What changes is the description of the signal: real, mostly idiosyncratic, and much steadier once factor moves are removed. Residual reversal at k = 5 is the natural candidate for the one-shot test of the reserved 2025-2026 period, specified here before anyone has looked at that data.")]));

children.push(P("Later note (Section 3.68): confirmed out of sample on July to December 2024, before the reserved period: k = 5 IC -0.0245, t -2.21, 73% of the size above. The cost verdict is unchanged."));

children.push(H2("3.51 Jensen's inequality in expected shortfall (Sadr, Mathematical Techniques in Finance, A.2.4) - a units error in shipped code"));
children.push(P("Sadr's Mathematical Techniques in Finance is a quantitative-finance primer, and most of it prices instruments this project does not have: bond math and yield curves (Ch. 2), forwards and futures (Ch. 4), binomial and Black-Scholes-Merton option pricing, Greeks and implied volatility (Ch. 5-6, whose risk-neutral side Section 3.49 already covers), and interest-rate derivatives (Ch. 7). Chapter 3 and Appendix A are general, and one line of the appendix lands on shipped code."));
children.push(H3("The error"));
children.push(P("Expected shortfall is the AVERAGE return in the worst 5% tail. Every estimator in var_models.py and the app's risk_metrics.empirical_risk (the 'Expected shortfall (worst 5%)' tile, Section 3.21) computed it as expm1(mean(log tail)), the geometric mean of the tail's gross returns. By Jensen's inequality, exp(E[X]) <= E[exp(X)], so this is always more negative than the average loss it is labelled as. The ES backtest made it a units mismatch too: backtest_es scores the forecast against SIMPLE realised returns. A tail of -10% and -30% log returns averages a 17.7% loss, not the 18.1% the old formula reported. VaR was never affected: a quantile commutes with exp. The error is specific to taking a mean across the change of scale."));
children.push(H3("How large"));
children.push(table([2400, 1900, 1900, 1900, 1860], [
  ["(run_es_jensen.py, 5% tail)", "h = 1", "h = 5", "h = 20", "h = 60"],
  ["RELIANCE, old -> new", "-3.99% -> -3.97%", "-8.97% -> -8.91%", "-15.25% -> -14.88%", "-19.88% -> -19.42%"],
  ["TCS, gap / ES", "0.3%", "0.6%", "1.0%", "0.7%"],
  ["TATASTEEL, gap / ES", "0.3%", "0.4%", "1.4%", "0.6%"],
  ["YESBANK, gap / ES", "3.5%", "2.8%", "1.1%", "0.4%"],
]));
children.push(P([B("Small, always conservative, and largest where the tail is most dispersed. "), T("Nobody was misled into under-reserving. But the tile overstated the average bad outcome by up to a few percent of itself, and the number was not the quantity on its label.")]));
children.push(H3("The fix"));
children.push(P("Sample-based ES (historical simulation, FHS, the generative sampler, and the app's empirical_risk) is now mean(expm1(tail)), through one helper, var_models._tail_es. Gaussian ES uses the lognormal tail mean, exp(mu + sigma^2/2) x Phi(z - sigma) / alpha. Cornish-Fisher's documented approximation carries the same log-to-simple correction as the Gaussian of the same sigma. Re-running Section 3.25's ES backtest on its fat-tailed series, the Gaussian model is still rejected (severity ratio 1.88, p = 0.0015, unchanged) and historical simulation still passes (1.02, p = 0.125, was 1.01, p = 0.16). Two new tests pin the definition, for every sample-based estimator and for the app's tile."));
children.push(H3("What was checked and left alone"));
children.push(P("The point forecast is a median and labelled one: under a lognormal, exp(mean log) is the median price, simulate_price_paths reports the median of its paths, and the UI calls it the median forecast, so Jensen's inequality is respected by the labels. Kelly's ratio and gambler's ruin (Ch. 3.6.5) are declined: Sadr's binary-bet Kelly fraction f* = 2p - 1 sizes every bet at zero with hit rates indistinguishable from 50%, and the gambler's-ruin formula is Section 3.40's conclusion in closed form. The sizing the app does support scales by risk, which needs no edge. Performance measures are in strategy_metrics.py (Treynor and Jensen's alpha need a market beta a market-neutral book does not have); z-scores, mean reversion and PCA are Sections 3.39 and 3.50; CAPM and Markowitz are portfolio allocation, declined before; the random walk is the baseline every model here is tested against."));
children.push(P("The rest of the code was searched for the same pattern. strategy_metrics.evaluate_strategy converts log returns to simple before compounding, and HAR-RV's log-variance back-transform already carries a bias correction. The long-short books in Sections 3.40 and 3.50 average each leg's LOG returns, while an equal-weighted leg earns the mean of its simple returns, so a log book understates a reversal book. run_residual_reversal.py now reports both: raw gross +0.025% log vs +0.037% simple (breakeven 1.4 vs 2.1 bp); residual k = 5 +0.263% vs +0.263% (breakeven 15.1 vs 15.2 bp). The gap moves no verdict: 15.2 bp is still below STT's 20 bp round trip, and Section 3.40's 5.0 bp becomes about 7.5 bp on Section 3.46's simple-return rebuild."));
children.push(P([B("The second error a book has found in shipped code, "), T("after Section 3.49. Like that one, it was a change of scale applied without noticing what it does to the quantity on the other side.")]));

children.push(H2("3.52 A p-value is not the probability of no edge (Kanungo, Probabilistic Machine Learning for Finance and Investing, Ch. 4)"));
children.push(P("Kanungo argues that finance should model with probability distributions rather than point estimates. Most of that argument is already how this product works: its forecasts are distributions, its intervals are checked for coverage, and its headline output is uncertainty rather than direction. One chapter lands on something the app shows on every row."));
children.push(H3("The inverse fallacy, on the confidence badge"));
children.push(P("The Basis column justifies each badge with its Diebold-Mariano p-value, e.g. 'weak edge (DM p=0.14)'. A p-value is P(a result this good | no edge), not P(no edge | this result); Ch. 4 calls reading one as the other the prosecutor's fallacy. Sellke, Bayarri & Berger (2001) bound the gap: the Bayes factor for the null is at least -e p ln p (for p < 1/e). From even odds that gives a floor on the probability of no edge:"));
children.push(table([3000, 3300, 3660], [
  ["DM p", "Naive misreading, P(no edge) = p", "Floor on P(no edge)"],
  ["0.01", "1%", "11.1%"],
  ["0.05", "5%", "28.9%"],
  ["0.10 (the High threshold)", "10%", "38.5%"],
  ["0.14", "14%", "42.8%"],
  ["0.20 (the Medium threshold)", "20%", "46.7%"],
]));
children.push(P("The floor is the alternative's best case, so the true probability can only be higher, and it ignores that several models were tried (Section 3.16's job)."));
children.push(H3("What changed"));
children.push(P("backtest.min_bayes_factor and backtest.null_probability_floor are new pure functions. gating.confidence_reason appends the floor wherever the badge claims an edge: 'beats random walk (DM p=0.05, cov 94%; P(no edge) >= 29%)' and 'weak edge (DM p=0.14; P(no edge) >= 43%)'. Low and No edge rows claim nothing and are unchanged. The badge rule itself is not changed: it is validated live against realised skill, and re-tuning its thresholds to a prior would be a new, unvalidated rule. Three tests in test_gates.py pin the bound, the floors at the bucket thresholds, and the exact Basis strings."));
children.push(H3("The rest of the book"));
children.push(P("Ch. 1-2 (model error, the three kinds of uncertainty) are background: aleatory uncertainty is the cone, epistemic was tested in Section 3.34, ontological is the novelty gate of Section 3.26. Ch. 3's Monte Carlo and tail-risk points are the simulated paths and Sections 3.25 and 3.51. Ch. 4's interval fallacies were checked against the README and UI: the 95% band is always described as covering ~95% of outcomes, checked empirically. Ch. 5-6 need earnings data. Ch. 8's generative VaR/ES is var_models; the paper portfolio already reports compounded, time-average growth, which is the ergodicity point; Kelly sizes every bet at zero without an edge."));
children.push(P([B("Chapter 7 is declined, and its evaluation is worth recording. "), T("The ensemble predicts Apple's daily excess return from the SAME DAY's S&P 500 excess return, so its out-of-sample 69% R-squared on 10 test days measures co-movement, not forecasting. It also treats the ^TNX quote (a yield in percent) as a fraction, turning a 3.8% yield into 380% a year, and subtracts that from returns already multiplied by 100. The concern behind it, parameter uncertainty in short samples, is what the conformal intervals answer with a finite-sample coverage guarantee that a 21-day Bayesian posterior does not carry.")]));

children.push(H2("3.53 Hilpisch's technical rules, optimised and tested (Python for Algorithmic Trading, Ch. 4) - the Probability of Backtest Overfitting"));
children.push(P("Python for Algorithmic Trading is mostly about the machinery of trading: environments, data storage, sockets, the Oanda and FXCM broker APIs, and deployment. Its method chapter, Ch. 4, backtests three classic rules (SMA crossover, momentum, mean reversion) and optimises each by brute force for in-sample gross performance - 500 SMA pairs for the crossover. Its own Data Snooping and Overfitting section says the examples are chosen to make a point, cites Bailey et al. (2015), and leaves the validation to the reader. This section does that validation on NSE."));
children.push(H3("New: the Probability of Backtest Overfitting"));
children.push(P("strategy_metrics.probability_of_backtest_overfitting implements CSCV (Bailey, Borwein, Lopez de Prado & Zhu): cut the history into 16 blocks; for each of the 12,870 ways to use half as in-sample, pick the configuration with the best in-sample Sharpe and see where it ranks on the other half. PBO is the share of splits where the winner lands at or below the out-of-sample median: about 0.5 with no skill, near 0 for a genuine edge, above 0.5 when selection picks noise that reverses. Five tests pin those regimes. The paper's performance-degradation slope is deliberately not returned: with complementary halves it is mechanically negative even for a genuine edge (the first draft of the test measured -0.94 on one truly better configuration)."));
children.push(H3("The test"));
children.push(P("run_technical_rules.py: 47 of the 50 most liquid names, survivorship-free, 2018-2024 (before the reserved test period), the book's 500 SMA pairs, momentum m in {1..250} (10 values), mean reversion SMA x threshold (20 configurations, threshold relative to the SMA because the book's price-unit threshold does not transfer across stocks); long-short as in the book and long-only; next-day simple returns, 10 bp one way."));
children.push(table([2600, 1500, 1300, 1300, 1400, 1860], [
  ["Rule", "Best in-sample, gross", "Same, net", "Buy & hold", "Median PBO", "OOS pick beats B&H"],
  ["SMA, long-short", "+153%", "+55%", "+131%", "0.47", "19%"],
  ["SMA, long-only", "+131%", "+130%", "+131%", "0.44", "26%"],
  ["Momentum, long-short", "+306%", "+109%", "+131%", "0.45", "11%"],
  ["Momentum, long-only", "+225%", "+163%", "+131%", "0.42", "21%"],
  ["Mean reversion, long-short", "+6%", "-20%", "+131%", "0.42", "21%"],
  ["Mean reversion, long-only", "+37%", "+28%", "+131%", "0.52", "15%"],
]));
children.push(P([B("The book's method looks fine before costs; nothing survives the honest version. "), T("Optimised on the first half and traded on the second, every family's pick trails buy & hold (out-of-sample Sharpe 0.91) and beats it on at most a quarter of names. Momentum long-short falls from +0.68 to +0.03 out of sample, matching its pooled PBO of 0.65. Mean reversion long-short has the lowest pooled PBO (0.25) and loses money out of sample in 94% of splits: selection reliably finds the least bad of a losing family. The book's own defaults (SMA 42/252, momentum 3), with no selection, beat buy & hold on 11-34% of names. The winning SMA pair differs from stock to stock - none is best on more than 3 of 47.")]));
children.push(P("The rest of the book: environments are covered by the pins and run manifest; the bhavcopy cache needs no other store; Ch. 5's direction models are the model zoo; Ch. 6's event-based classes are Section 3.46; sockets and broker APIs do not apply to a forecasting app that places no orders; Kelly and optimal leverage need an edge (Section 3.51); monitoring is the prediction log. Two of the book\'s evaluations are worth knowing before reusing them. Ch. 5\'s logistic regression predicts up on all 1,983 in-sample days, so its 53.3% hit ratio is the share of up days and its strategy is buy & hold. Ch. 10\'s Kelly leverage (half Kelly above 50) is estimated on the test period and applied to the same period, and its leveraged equity curve multiplies LOG returns by leverage, which drops the -l^2 sigma^2/2 variance drag the Kelly formula exists to balance; this project scales simple returns, which is correct. One correctness note: the book's maximum drawdown is cummax - cumret in gross-performance points, not a fraction of the peak; strategy_metrics.max_drawdown is already relative."));

children.push(H2("3.54 The 52-week high (Yan, Python for Finance, Ch. 8) - the book has the sign backwards, and the data cannot settle it"));
children.push(P("Python for Finance is an introduction to Python with option pricing and volatility chapters. One item in its Ch. 8 list of applications had never been tested here: the 52-week-high strategy. Yan states it as contrarian (long near the 52-week low, short near the high); George & Hwang (2004) found the opposite. Both were written down before running. It was worth testing because a year-long high moves slowly, so unlike Section 3.39's reversal it should trade little, and its long side can be bought without shorting."));
children.push(P("run_52week_high.py: Section 3.39's survivorship-free 400-name universe, 2018-2024 (before the reserved period), 1,328 signal days. Signal price / 52-week high; 21-session horizon; Fama-MacBeth IC with Newey-West; decile books rebalanced every 21 sessions and averaged over all 21 starting days; deciles are value thresholds with ties included, because in a rising market over a tenth of names sit exactly at their high and averaged ranks left the top decile empty."));
children.push(table([3300, 1400, 2100, 1500, 1660], [
  ["Measure", "Mean", "Across starting days", "t", "Breakeven"],
  ["Rank IC, price / 52-week high", "+0.0388", "-", "+1.85", "-"],
  ["Same, beyond momentum and 21-day return", "+0.0380", "-", "+1.70", "-"],
  ["Long-short decile, per month", "+1.07%", "+0.41% .. +1.71%", "<= +1.56", "118 bp"],
  ["Long side vs universe", "+0.17%", "-0.39% .. +0.70%", "median +0.36", "26 bp"],
  ["Short side: universe vs bottom decile", "+0.90%", "+0.66% .. +1.22%", "<= +1.79", "323 bp"],
]));
children.push(P([B("The sign is George & Hwang's, not Yan's, but it is not established. "), T("It is positive on both measures, beyond momentum, and on every starting day, so nothing supports the contrarian rule as the book states it. But the IC's t is 1.85 and the long-short book's t is at most 1.56: its 1.07% a month comes with about 9% monthly volatility over 63 months, so the gross is not distinguishable from zero and the 118 bp breakeven means nothing. The consistent part is the short side - stocks far below their highs keep lagging - which is the side this market will not let you trade. Not adopted and not discarded: it is the second natural candidate for a one-shot test of the reserved period, after Section 3.50's residual reversal, with a Bonferroni alpha/2 if both are tested.")]));
children.push(P("Two errors in the book's Ch. 8: the 52-week rule's direction, and a January-effect test that runs Bartlett's test (equal variances), reads it as a T-value, and concludes there is no January effect - a claim about means. With six Januaries in the window a mean test would have a standard error near 2.4%, so it is not run. The rest of the book is covered elsewhere: Roll and Amihud in microstructure.py, Fama-MacBeth in strategy_metrics, VaR in var_models, the GARCH family in run_vol_comparison.py; options need a chain this project does not have."));

children.push(H2("3.55 Monte Carlo noise in the cone (Hilpisch, Python for Finance, Ch. 12) - variance reduction is for means, not tails"));
children.push(P("The fourth Hilpisch book reviewed here; most of it is covered already (Ch. 15-16 are Python for Algorithmic Trading Ch. 4, 5 and 10, Section 3.53; Ch. 14 is the FXCM API; Ch. 17-20 are the DX derivatives library, which needs an option chain; Ch. 1-9 are tooling). The new material is in Ch. 10, 12, 13 and 15."));
children.push(P("The ARIMA/ARIMAX + GARCH cone is read off the 2.5th and 97.5th percentiles of 5,000 simulated paths. Ch. 12 shows antithetic variates and moment matching on the MEAN of a sample; the cone reads QUANTILES, so run_mc_variance_reduction.py measures how far the 2.5% edge moves between seeds, as a share of the band's half-width (2,000 seeds per cell)."));
children.push(table([2400, 1400, 1900, 1900, 2360], [
  ["Draws", "Paths", "Plain", "Antithetic", "Moment-matched"],
  ["Normal", "5,000", "1.96%", "1.87%", "1.44%"],
  ["Student-t(4)", "1,000", "7.04%", "6.85%", "6.31%"],
  ["Student-t(4)", "5,000", "3.05%", "3.08%", "3.21%"],
  ["Student-t(4)", "20,000", "1.58%", "1.54%", "1.88%"],
]));
children.push(P([B("At 5,000 paths the edge moves 2-3% of the half-width, and neither trick helps a fat-tailed quantile. "), T("Antithetic variates do not change how many draws land in the tail; moment matching injects the noise of the sample standard deviation, which is large when tails are heavy, and makes t(4) quantiles worse. The seed is fixed, so a given forecast is reproducible - true only since Section 3.60, because np.random.seed never reached arch\'s own generator. One fix: simulate_price_paths' last-resort fallback simulated only 1,000 paths (twice the edge noise) and now uses N_SIMS.")]));
children.push(P([B("Ch. 15's narrative contradicts its own output twice. "), T("For the sequential split the text says only GNB and LR achieve positive absolute performance; the output shows LR 0.963 and GNB 0.941 (both losses) and SVM 1.049 (the only gain). For the randomized split the text says SVM again performs best; its 0.695 is the worst row, below buy & hold's 0.878. The randomized split itself is the shuffled-split leakage Section 3.7's purged walk-forward prevents.")]));
children.push(P("The rest of the new material: Numba/Cython would not move a run time spent fitting models; simulated VaR is Section 3.25 and there is no counterparty for a CVA; normality tests were declined in Section 3.46; portfolio optimisation and pairs relationships were declined before; the random-walk hypothesis is the baseline every model here is tested against."));

children.push(H2("3.56 Cost of trade (Kanwar & Gupta, Python for Trading on Technical) - futures do not rescue reversal, and a train/serve skew in shipped code"));
children.push(P("A short TA-Lib cookbook: indicator recipes, candlestick patterns, one SMA x RSI setup, and a page on the cost of trading in India. That page is the useful one; following it up also turned up an error in shipped code."));
children.push(H3("An itemised cost schedule"));
children.push(P("strategy_metrics.india_round_trip_bps itemises the statutory charges from a dated schedule, so a backtest pays the rate in force at the time: cash delivery 22.22 bp a round trip (STT 20.00, stamp 1.50, exchange 0.59, SEBI 0.02, GST 0.11), intraday 3.53 bp, stock futures 1.67 bp before April 2023, 1.92 bp from April 2023, 2.63 bp from October 2024 and 5.63 bp from April 2026, when Budget 2026 raised futures STT from 0.02% to 0.05%. A first version written from memory missed that rise; the rates were then checked against exchange and broker notices. Delivery cost about 13x futures when the book was written and about 4x today. Sources checked: exchange and broker notices on the October 2024 revision (Zerodha bulletin of 28 Sep 2024; Business Standard, 1 Oct 2024), the April 2026 STT rise (Outlook Money) and uniform stamp duty from July 2020 (Zerodha bulletin, 1 Jul 2020); full links in README Section 55. The rates are statutory and change with budgets, so re-verify after the next one. The book's own futures arithmetic sums to about 1.7 bp (0.017%), but its text says approx .0002% and its code charges 0.01% per side."));
children.push(H3("Does trading through futures rescue reversal?"));
children.push(P("bhavcopy.fno_members reads the exchange's F&O bhavcopy on the first trading day of each month, so the list is the one that existed then (211 stocks in January 2018, 140 in January 2021, 183 in January 2024). Section 3.40 had called today's list an upper bound because the list was narrower historically; it was wider for most of the window (129-210 of the panel's names, median 147, against today's 131). run_futures_reversal.py re-runs the pre-registered k = 5 residual reversal on point-in-time F&O names only."));
children.push(table([3600, 3100, 3260], [
  ["", "Full universe (Section 3.50)", "F&O names, point-in-time"],
  ["Rank IC", "-0.0335 (t -8.08)", "-0.0144 (t -2.57)"],
  ["Gross per rebalance", "+0.263%", "+0.091%"],
  ["Breakeven, round trip", "15.2 bp", "5.2 bp"],
  ["Net t, futures at the rate in force + 2 bp spread", "-", "median -0.01"],
  ["Net t, futures at today's rate (5.6 bp)", "-", "median -0.21"],
]));
children.push(P([B("Futures do not rescue it. "), T("The signal lives mostly in names without futures; on the names you can short and trade cheaply it is less than half as strong and breaks even at 5.2 bp: about 3 bp above the statutory charges in force during 2018-2024, which a realistic spread all but removes, and below today\'s 5.6 bp statutory cost alone.")]));
children.push(H3("A train/serve skew in shipped code"));
children.push(P("direct_forecast.compute_origin_row built the forecast-origin row from prices and volumes only, so atr_norm and range_pct at the origin were close-to-close proxies while every training row used high/low true range - for XGBoost/LightGBM, the Ensemble and the Quantile model. Measured on NSE daily origins, the served atr_norm was 46% below the trained one and range_pct 20% above it. The intraday parity test checked six columns and nothing else. _features_from_buffers now takes the bars and computes true range exactly as training does; test_every_feature_matches_between_training_and_serving pins every column. run_origin_skew.py shows what the skew was worth: 5-day XGBoost forecasts moved by 1-9% of their own size with no sign flips in 60, so this is a correctness fix and the published verdicts stand."));
children.push(P([B("A duplicate feature, removed. "), T("bb_position was exactly price_zscore / 2 (correlation 1.0). Any Bollinger position is linear in the z-score. run_bb_position_removal.py measured the removal against a re-seeding control at 60 origins: it moved forecasts by 0.14-0.18% against 0.17% from re-seeding alone, with accuracy changing by noise in both directions - a cleanliness change, not an accuracy one. Section 3.36's figures were measured on the 26-feature set and stay as recorded.")]));
children.push(H3("The book's indicators and its setup"));
children.push(P("run_kanwar_setup.py: with the book\'s own TA-Lib calls and periods (a first version used hand-written approximations and was corrected), the indicators overlap the existing features at CCI(14) 0.93, slow Stochastic 0.87, Stochastic RSI 0.87, Balance of Power (9-day SMA) 0.72, OBV change 0.72 and Chaikin ADOSC 0.63. None is added: the first three are near-copies, the last three partly new but another trial against a zoo with no edge. The rule as the book\'s text states it (enter RSI > 70, exit RSI < 50) differs from its code and does worse: +37% against buy & hold\'s +192%, beating it on none of 7 names. The book\'s own results table cannot show the setup works: its Percent_Return divides price points earned by the FINAL price, which is not a return on any capital, and it has no buy-and-hold row - by the same metric, holding RELIANCE over 2010-2021 earned more than the strategy\'s 62%. The SMA x RSI setup (20 configurations, 7 of the book's 8 names), charged at cash delivery because it is long-only, loses to buy & hold even in-sample (+149% vs +192%), has a pooled PBO of 0.79, and optimised on the first half falls from a net Sharpe of +1.23 to +0.45 against buy & hold's +1.00, beating it on 14% of names."));

children.push(H3("The indicator chapters\' own trading rules"));
children.push(P("The indicator chapters each end with a rule shown on INFY 2018-20 (two of them on image pages missed on the first pass): long while MACD(12,26,9) is above its signal line; buy when the 9-day-smoothed Balance of Power crosses above zero, sell below; buy on a slow-Stochastic %K-over-%D cross with %D < 20, sell on the reverse with %D > 80. run_kanwar_indicator_rules.py reproduces the book\'s MACD trade count on INFY (24 trades against the book\'s 26, from adjusted exchange bars rather than its Yahoo file), then runs each rule unchanged on the 50 most liquid names, 2018-2024, long-only, at cash-delivery cost. MACD shows a timing return of +2.3% a year against buy & hold held for the same share of days, but it comes from one episode, the COVID crash: without February-June 2020 it is -0.9% a year (t 0.77). Balance of Power and the Stochastic rule pick their days no better than chance, and none of the three beats buy & hold on more than 44% of names."));
children.push(H3("Candlestick patterns, tested"));
children.push(P("run_candlesticks.py runs the book's five TA-Lib patterns (validation-only dependency) as an event study on the 400-name panel: signed 5-day excess return, Newey-West t, Bonferroni bar 2.58. Close-to-close, Marubozu (t +6.51) and Evening Star (t +2.66) look right and Inverted Hammer looks wrong-way (t -3.10). Three diagnostics added afterwards - entering at the next open, dropping price-band-locked bars, keeping the liquid half - show the Inverted Hammer effect is a closing-price artefact (t -0.29 at the next open), Evening Star falls below the bar (t 1.78), and Marubozu's continuation halves to +0.25% per 5-day trade at the next open (t 3.08), about +2 bp after cash delivery costs, and is +0.17% (t 1.90) in the liquid half. No pattern is a usable signal."));

children.push(H2("3.57 Long memory and wavelets (Mariani & Florescu, Quantitative Finance, Ch. 5, 12-13) - persistence that is the estimator\'s, and a forecast that is the future"));
children.push(P("Quantitative Finance is mostly derivatives pricing (Black-Scholes, Heston, SABR, CIR, PDEs, trees, finite differences, bonds, CDS, CDOs), which needs an option chain this project does not have. Its data-facing chapters make two measurable claims: a Hurst exponent above 0.5 is persistence that enables prediction (p. 334), and the further the Hurst and DFA exponents are from 0.5, the stronger the evidence of a crash waiting to happen (p. 357)."));
children.push(H3("New: long_memory.py"));
children.push(P("rs_hurst implements the book\'s R/S slope and, with corrected=True, the Anis-Lloyd-Peters correction; lo_modified_rs is Lo\'s (1991) test, which absorbs short-range dependence; dfa is order-1 DFA, with the book\'s |returns| variant; fgn generates fractional Gaussian noise with a known H. Calibrated first: on independent noise the book\'s R/S reads 0.567 (corrected 0.482, DFA 0.501); on an AR(1) with only short memory it reads 0.679, while Lo\'s test rejects at its nominal rate (6.7%); DFA recovers a known H (0.681 for H = 0.7) and reads 1.494 on random-walk levels. Eight tests pin this."));
children.push(H3("On NSE returns"));
children.push(table([4200, 2800, 2960], [
  ["Median across 183 names", "On returns", "Same returns, shuffled"],
  ["Book R/S Hurst", "0.566", "0.561"],
  ["Corrected H", "0.489", "-"],
  ["Standard DFA of returns", "0.514", "-"],
  ["Book DFA of |returns|", "0.713", "0.502"],
]));
children.push(P([B("The persistence in returns is the estimator\'s; the memory in volatility is real. "), T("The book\'s R/S reads the same with all memory destroyed, and Lo\'s test rejects for 10% of names against 5% by chance, so there is no long memory in daily returns to forecast from. DFA of |returns| reads 0.713 against 0.502 shuffled: the volatility persistence HAR-RV is built on, which says nothing about direction.")]));
children.push(H3("The crash-warning claim, pre-specified"));
children.push(P("Every 21 sessions, the book\'s |H - 0.5| and |alpha - 0.5| on each name\'s trailing year, against the worst drawdown over the next 21 sessions; 64 months; bar positive and |t| > 2.24 beyond trailing 60-day volatility. Both passed (t 2.41 and 3.15). Diagnostics added afterwards, beyond 60- and 250-day volatility, volatility of volatility, the past year\'s return and drawdown: the R/S signal disappears (t 0.97) and the DFA signal survives barely (t 2.12, above 2 but below the pre-set 2.24), at a twentieth of trailing volatility\'s predictive strength - volatility persistence, not a crash waiting to happen. Does it improve the app\'s volatility forecast? run_har_dfa.py tests it, pre-specified: HAR-RV fitted exactly as the app does on 97 names, an expanding-window out-of-sample recalibration with and without alpha, scored by QLIKE over 40 origins and 4,837 forecasts. HAR-RV\'s own forecast scores 0.2831, a pooled recalibration 0.3276, and adding alpha 0.3396 - 3.7% worse (DM t -1.67), with a coefficient that swings from -2.46 to +0.42 between fits. Not adopted; the shipped HAR-RV, with its per-name level correction, beats both re-fits."));
children.push(H3("Wavelet forecasting: the leak, measured"));
children.push(P("One rule - tomorrow\'s direction from the slope of the wavelet-denoised log price (Haar, 4 levels, cycle-spun, soft universal threshold) - run two ways on 19 liquid names, 24,436 forecasts: denoising the whole series once gives a 61.8% hit rate (t +36.9); denoising only the trailing 256 days at each date gives 50.1% (t +0.26). All of the apparent skill is the future leaking in through the transform. (A first version reported 2% and 14% because thresholded Haar is a staircase whose slope is zero on most days and the zeros were scored as misses; cycle spinning and scoring only real calls fixed it.)"));
children.push(P("The rest of the book: options and bonds need instruments this project does not have; variance reduction was Section 3.55; stochastic volatility and jumps are covered by HAR-RV, GARCH and the conformal cone; factors were Section 3.50; copulas model joint tails across assets, a portfolio question a single-stock app does not ask."));

children.push(H2("3.58 Synthetic returns (Hilpisch, Reinforcement Learning for Finance, Ch. 5) - a test that a shuffle passes, and what the project\'s own generators keep"));
children.push(P("The book teaches deep Q-learning for trading, hedging, allocation and execution. Reinforcement learning was declined in Section 3.27 and every review since - a directional policy with no directional edge to learn - and the book\'s own Ch. 3 names the same obstacles: limited data and no market impact. Its Part II answers limited data by generating more, and Ch. 5 validates GAN-generated returns with a Kolmogorov-Smirnov test (25 of 25 pass), concluding they have all the qualities of the original data (p. 80). KS compares only the one-day marginal distribution: it cannot see volatility clustering, and a shuffled copy of the real series passes it perfectly."));
children.push(H3("New: synthetic_check.py"));
children.push(P("compare_paths reports the KS test next to four stylized facts it cannot see - volatility clustering, the 1% tail, the leverage effect and the kurtosis of 20-period sums - each as a synthetic-to-real ratio; stationary_bootstrap_path reproduces var_models\' generator as a path; ks_2samp is pure numpy and matches SciPy (a first version returned p = 0 for identical samples and was fixed). Seven tests pin it, centrally that a shuffle of a clustered series passes KS and is still flagged."));
children.push(table([3400, 1300, 1500, 1200, 1500, 1060], [
  ["Generator (5 NSE names, medians)", "Passes KS", "Clustering kept", "1% tail", "20-day kurtosis kept", "Matches all"],
  ["Shuffle", "100%", "-0.02", "1.00", "0.06", "0%"],
  ["Gaussian", "0%", "0.01", "0.92", "-0.02", "0%"],
  ["GAN (project\'s optional)", "40%", "-0.03", "0.87", "0.05", "0%"],
  ["Block bootstrap (project\'s default)", "96%", "0.20", "1.00", "0.12", "0%"],
  ["GARCH(1,1)-t", "100%", "0.45", "1.00", "0.44", "16%"],
]));
children.push(P([B("The chapter\'s test is blind where expected, and the project\'s default generator was described wrongly. "), T("The shuffle passes KS on every name while keeping none of the clustering (5 seeded paths per name; a first version used one unseeded path, and its GARCH row changed between runs). The project\'s GAN keeps none of the time structure. The block bootstrap\'s docstring said it preserves the volatility clustering; at its mean block of 5 it keeps about a fifth (0.20; a block of 40 keeps ~0.6 of the clustering and ~0.85 of the 20-day kurtosis). Tested on the job it does - multi-day VaR coverage on 10 names - the longer block did not separate from the default (1% 5-day exception rate 1.59% vs 1.36%, identical at 10 days and at 5%), so the default stays and Section 3.25\'s numbers stand; the docstring now states what the generator keeps.")]));
children.push(H3("What did the book\'s trading agent learn? A constant rule, on the same simulator"));
children.push(P("Ch. 6 reports that its trained deep Q-learning agent never loses money (mean gross performance 1.582, accuracy about 0.55). It is trained and tested on simulated prices from a mean-reverting process with x0 = 100, kappa = 2, theta = 50, sigma = 0.1 over 504 steps - a price built to fall from 100 toward 50, with noise far smaller than the decline. run_rl_simulation_check.py replicates the simulator exactly and scores rules that learn nothing over 1,000 episodes."));
children.push(table([3800, 1600, 2400, 2160], [
  ["Book\'s simulator", "Accuracy", "Mean gross performance", "Episodes losing"],
  ["The book\'s trained agent", "~0.549", "1.582", "none"],
  ["Always short (one constant action)", "0.579", "1.920", "0.0%"],
  ["Short above theta", "0.583", "1.959", "0.0%"],
  ["Random", "0.500", "1.012", "49.9%"],
  ["Control kappa = 0: always short", "0.501", "1.025", "46.8%"],
]));
children.push(P([B("A rule that learns nothing beats the trained agent on the book\'s own simulator, "), T("and nothing earns anything once the built-in drift is removed. The agent\'s result is a property of the simulated price, not something learned about markets - and an agent trained on a simulator learns the simulator, including any predictability its parameters put there.")]));
children.push(P("The rest of the book: Bayesian updating is Section 3.52\'s point; deep Q-learning is declined; noise-augmented training data is another regulariser for models already regularised, not worth another trial (the book\'s own noise-trained agent tests at accuracies of 0.449-0.561, around a coin flip); hedging needs options; allocation was declined; optimal execution (Almgren-Chriss) matters only once a strategy has an edge to execute, and none survives the statutory costs of Section 3.56."));

children.push(H2("3.59 Cross-check of Sections 3.51-3.58 - what was re-verified, and five defects it found"));
children.push(P("Everything added in Sections 3.51-3.58 was cross-checked in four layers: pyflakes on all 44 new or changed files (no undefined names; four lint issues in new drivers fixed); the full suite and tools/check.py with its test-count mode (623 passed); an offline end-to-end run of the XGBoost, LightGBM, Quantile and HAR-RV forecasts on real NSE bars (all finite and ordered) with all 53 app modules compiled; and all 19 validation drivers re-run against the figures this document quotes - 18 reproduced every figure (141 in all), one did not."));
children.push(table([2600, 4000, 3360], [
  ["Where", "Defect", "Fix (each pinned by a test)"],
  ["ML_model._features_from_buffers", "A missing high or low on the forecast bar put NaN into range_pct at the origin", "Falls back to the close-to-close proxy when the true-range value is not finite"],
  ["strategy_metrics.india_charges", "Dates before 2018-01-01 returned an empty schedule and raised", "The base schedule always applies; later revisions are date-gated"],
  ["bhavcopy.fno_members", "A network failure was cached as an empty month and never retried", "Only real snapshots are cached; all 78 cached months verified real"],
  ["console._write", "The badge\'s >= printed as ? on a cp1252 Windows console", "Reason-code symbols are spelled out in ASCII first"],
  ["run_synthetic_check.py", "Unseeded GARCH simulation and one path per name: the GARCH row changed every run, and the bootstrap\'s 0.35 was one draw", "Seeded draws, median of 5 paths per name; Section 3.58 corrected (bootstrap keeps 0.20 of the clustering)"],
]));
children.push(P("No other unseeded randomness was found in the new drivers or modules. The first three defects are edge cases the measured results never hit; the fifth affected a published figure, which has been corrected - the conclusions held, and the bootstrap\'s shortfall is larger than first reported."));

children.push(H2("3.60 Full audit of Sections 3.2-3.58 - every module read, every offline driver re-run, and what it found"));
children.push(P("Section 3.59 covered Sections 3.51-3.58. This audit covers the whole project: all 53 app modules and the validation harness read line by line against the books in Books/Implemented; pyflakes over every module, test and tool (no undefined names); the full suite (642 passed - 623 before plus 19 that pin the fixes); and the 10 offline bhavcopy drivers for Sections 3.39-3.47 re-run against every figure quoted. Sections 3.42, 3.45 and 3.47 reproduce exactly. The 18 drivers that read live yfinance data were not re-run: their data now runs to October 2026, so a re-run would move every figure and read the reserved 2025-onward holdout, which needs the owner\'s approval."));
children.push(table([2400, 3900, 3560], [
  ["Where", "Defect", "Fix and effect"],
  ["ARIMAX_model", "pmdarima 2.x silently ignored the exogenous= keyword: ARIMAX fitted no regressors and was plain ARIMA everywhere", "Regressors in X, lagged one bar (same-bar would have been 1.217x the random walk at 83% coverage). Honest ARIMAX 1.030x vs 0.998x without - regressors cost ~3%. Meta-ensemble keeps use_exog=False, its validated member"],
  ["model_utils.simulate_price_paths", "arch draws from its own generator; np.random.seed never reached it", "ARIMA/ARIMAX cones now reproducible run to run"],
  ["market_context (opt-in)", "Hourly bars got the day\'s 15:30 index close", "Intraday rows take the previous session"],
  ["iv_data (opt-in)", "Intraday origin read the day\'s India-VIX close", "Prior session"],
  ["prediction_log.resolve_outcomes", "Targets not yet printed were resolved at a stale or partial price, permanently", "Resolve only once history runs past the target"],
  ["tuning.nested_search", "2 of 5 holdout origins were inside the tuned region", "score_from contract; tuning now generalises on 1 of 5 names, not 4"],
  ["feature_drift", "Permutation null ignored serial dependence: 97-100% false alarms on rolling features", "Block permutation: 0.02-0.05, full power kept; Section 3.36\'s volatility-drift reading withdrawn"],
  ["autoencoder_regime", "Windows included the raw price, so trend read as novelty", "Eight scale-free columns: median flags 21%/8% -> 13%/5%"],
  ["range_volatility.efficiency", "Squared-return baseline on raw close; splits flattered the range estimators", "Adjusted close: 1.7-2.4x, not 1.6-2.5x"],
  ["chronos_model", "Series id fetched both exchange listings on every call", "The company name is the id; no network"],
]));
children.push(P("Validation drivers: run_range_volatility.py reported a significantly WIDER cone as \'every paired t inside +/-2\' (now both directions, and the published 12-name, 24-origin default with the names recorded); run_open_interest.py defaulted to 50 names where Section 3.42 used 60 (now 60, reproducing it exactly). Figures corrected in place: Section 3.39\'s headline table (bb_position removal and corporate-action revisions; the seed study reproduces, median t 2.40), Section 3.40\'s sweep rows, Section 3.41\'s efficiency range and A/B, Section 3.43\'s bias table and levels, Section 3.44\'s two calibration rows, and the claims the code defects invalidated in Sections 3.26, 3.28 and 3.36."));
children.push(P("Known and documented rather than changed: the dormant excess-return target (add_market_index) would calibrate bands on excess residuals but apply them to price; build_panel re-fetches corporate actions live, so offline drivers drift over time (later changed: Section 3.74 freezes the feed, and Section 3.75 found a larger problem in it); the novelty gate\'s adjacent reference block is not an exchangeable null; and a few small calibration optimisms (no embargo before the cross-sectional calibration block, the stacking ensemble\'s XGBoost early-stopping on its own out-of-fold block, the Kalman band\'s missing finite-sample step, Vol-LSTM\'s whole-window clipping, HAR-RV\'s coefficients fitted over its calibration block). During the audit run_cross_sectional_clf.py and run_class_weight_ab.py were re-run before it was noticed that their default fetcher is yfinance through today; that output read 2025-2026 data, was not used, and is not reported."));

children.push(H2("3.61 Stops, dividends and factors (Mladjenovic, Stock Investing For Dummies, Ch. 6, 15, 17) - a stop is hit twice as often as the panel implied, and the quoted price drops on the ex-date"));
children.push(P("Stock Investing For Dummies (7th ed., 2024) is a retail investor\'s guide; most of it is advice to a person rather than a technique for a model. Three chapters make claims this project can measure on NSE data, and each changed something in the app."));
children.push(H3("Ch. 17: where a stop goes, and how often it is hit"));
children.push(P("The book: \'most investors set a stop-loss amount at about 10 percent below\', 5% if nervous, trailing stops of 8% or 20-25% for volatile stocks, and beta to judge the trading range. Measured on 183 NSE names, 2018-2024, with intraday lows (run_stop_loss.py):"));
children.push(table([3000, 1150, 1150, 1150, 1150, 1150, 1110], [
  ["Hit within 21 sessions", "5%", "8%", "10%", "15%", "20%", "25%"],
  ["All names (median)", "55%", "34%", "25%", "10%", "4%", "3%"],
  ["Quietest fifth (27%/yr)", "38%", "20%", "11%", "3%", "1%", "1%"],
  ["Most volatile fifth (54%/yr)", "66%", "50%", "42%", "25%", "14%", "8%"],
]));
children.push(P("The default 10% stop is hit within a month one time in four, and four times as often on the volatile fifth as on the quiet fifth. Beta and volatility are closely related on NSE (Spearman +0.87), but volatility ranks names by stop-outs better (+0.92 vs +0.82): beta is correlation x relative volatility, not volatility."));
children.push(P([B("The end-point trap. "), T("The panel\'s P(loss > x%) is the chance the price ENDS below -x%; a stop is triggered if the price TOUCHES -x% at any point, which for a driftless random walk is twice as likely (the reflection principle). Scored out of sample on ~170,000 name-window-stop cases: in the 2020-21 crash, a 10% / 21-session stop was hit 28.7% of the time against an end-point estimate of 16.7% and a reflection estimate of 33.5%; calibration error end-point 0.086, reflection 0.040, the stock\'s own excursion history 0.044. In the calm 2022-24 market all three were 0.040-0.044, the reflection estimate a third too high because drift lowers stop-outs. When a 10% stop is hit, 9% of the time the price gaps through it; the mean sale is 0.24% below the stop.")]));
children.push(P([B("What shipped. "), T("The Risk & Probability panel shows P(a -x% stop is hit) = 2 x the end-point P(loss) (risk_metrics.stop_hit_probability), and the stop with a 1-in-5 chance of being hit within the horizon - the book\'s \'size the stop by volatility\' made quantitative.")]));
children.push(H3("Ch. 6: the ex-date drop, and two consequences"));
children.push(P("On 2,171 NSE cash dividends (run_dividend_dates.py; same-day pairs merged) the price falls by 81-94% of the dividend on the ex-date (median drop ratio 0.84, 0.81, 0.85 and 0.94 for yields 0.5-1%, 1-2%, 2-5% and 5%+; corrected in Section 3.69), as the book says. 42% of dividends are at least a tenth of a 5-day 95% cone\'s half-width."));
children.push(P([B("A prediction-log bias, fixed. "), T("Outcomes were taken from a fetch made later, which back-adjusts every bar before each new ex-date, so a dividend going ex after the target was recorded as a loss of the dividend yield. Outcomes are now the total return from origin to target in one fetch, anchor x adj(target) / adj(origin).")]));
children.push(P([B("An ex-dividend notice. "), T("Forecasts are total-return prices and cannot show the drop the user sees in the quote. corporate_events.dividend_notice reads announced ex-dates from NSE\'s corporate-action feed and, when one falls inside the horizon, the app states the dividend, its yield and the expected quoted drop (0.85 x the dividend) - not a forecast miss.")]));
children.push(H3("Ch. 15: the micro factors this data can build"));
children.push(P("Fama-MacBeth rank IC against the next 21 sessions\' excess return, non-overlapping monthly samples (63 months), Bonferroni |t| > 2.50 over four factors: dividend yield +0.0354 (t +2.91, significant; +2.59 within volatility quintiles), low volatility +0.0423 (+1.56), low beta +0.0230 (+0.71), liquidity +0.0070 (+0.33). Attacked, dividend yield is positive in both halves (t 2.09, 2.09), but is mostly whether a company pays at all (payer vs non-payer t 2.00); among payers, yield adds nothing (t 0.92). Closer to the book\'s quality factor than to \'high yield\'. A lead for a single pre-registered holdout test; nothing ships."));
children.push(P("Not taken: personal finance, brokers and US tax (Ch. 2, 7, 20); ratio and earnings-based factors (Ch. 9, 11-12, 21, App. B), blocked by data as factors (Section 3.33) but shown as live information in Section 3.62; trends, channels and RSI (Ch. 10), already tested in Sections 3.53 and 3.56; the 52-week high (Section 3.54); short selling (Section 3.40); stops as a strategy (Section 3.46); ETFs, sectors, IPOs, crises and AI prompting, which carry no testable technique on this data. 10 new tests."));

children.push(H2("3.62 A company check beside the forecast (Mladjenovic, Stock Investing For Dummies, Ch. 3, 4, 21, App. B) - the information the price models cannot see"));
children.push(P("Section 3.61 took the three claims in this book that NSE data can test. The rest is theory written for an investor, not a model: what a good company looks like, what to call a holding of a given length, and which risks exist. Its value here is as information shown to the user, and one gap makes it worth showing. Every model sees prices only; none can tell a company growing its profits from one whose liabilities exceed its assets, and the forecast looks the same for both. The book's tool for that blind spot is a checklist, so the app now shows one: a collapsed Company check expander below the Risk & Probability panel."));
children.push(P([B("It feeds nothing and is not a tested predictor. "), T("Testing it would need each company's statements as they stood on every past date, and Section 3.33 measured that yfinance holds about a year of that per NSE name. Showing today's statements for today's decision is the use fundamentals.current_snapshot was always allowed; joining them to a historical panel is the look-ahead as_of_join refuses. The expander says so in its caption.")]));
children.push(H3("Ch. 21: six of the ten hallmarks can be measured"));
children.push(P("stock_checklist.py scores the selected company from yfinance's latest annual statements, its dividend history and NSE's live F&O list. Every threshold is the book's, quoted on screen beside the result:"));
children.push(table([2000, 3560, 3800], [
  ["Hallmark", "Measured as", "The book's rule"],
  ["Rising profits", "Net income, latest year against the one before; years up out of the last three", "Rising; a loss is a red flag"],
  ["Rising sales", "Total revenue, the same way", "Three years of sales give a good overall gauge"],
  ["Low liabilities", "Total liabilities / net equity (App. B debt-to-net-equity)", "Above 1 isn't good"],
  ["Bargain price", "Trailing P/E (or price / latest EPS); P/B as the second route", "15 or less good value, 10-20 conservative, above 40 a speculation; no earnings, no bargain; at or below book value"],
  ["Growing dividends", "Trailing-12-month cash dividend per share against a year earlier", "A rising dividend is hard evidence of real profit"],
  ["Optionable", "In NSE's F&O segment, the only NSE stocks with listed options", "Options add ways to earn on, or protect, a holding"],
]));
children.push(P("The other four (a growing market, a high barrier to entry, a low political profile, favourable megatrends) have no data source, so they are listed as questions for the user, with the company's sector and industry, rather than guessed. The headline is 'k of n measurable hallmarks met', next to the book's own bar: a stock with half of the ten is a super-solid choice."));
children.push(H3("Appendix B: the ratio tests, grouped as the book groups them"));
children.push(table([1700, 2600, 5060], [
  ["Group", "Ratio", "Book's rule"],
  ["Profitability", "Return on equity (on average equity since Section 3.64)", "Above 10% is good"],
  ["Profitability", "Return on assets", "Flags an ROE made with debt (the book's example: 25% ROE on 5% ROA, a 5x gap)"],
  ["Profitability", "Net margin trend (common-size)", "Higher than last year (within half a point counts as unchanged)"],
  ["Solvency", "Quick ratio", "At least 1"],
  ["Solvency", "Debt to net equity", "At most 1"],
  ["Liquidity", "Current ratio", "2 or more desirable; under 1 a red flag"],
  ["Operating", "Sales / receivables", "Receivables outgrowing sales; flagged at a 20% fall in a year - THIS PROJECT'S cut-off, since the book gives an example, not a number"],
  ["Valuation", "Price / sales", "2 or less very undervalued, up to 4 fine, above 5 be very hesitant"],
  ["Valuation", "Price / book", "At or below 1 suits a value investor; above that, context only (not a showstopper)"],
]));
children.push(P("The book says a solid company should at minimum pass the profitability and solvency tests comfortably, so those two tallies are shown as metrics beside the hallmark count."));
children.push(H3("Three cases the book's rules get wrong as written"));
children.push(bullet([B("Negative net equity. "), T("Vodafone Idea's liabilities exceed its assets. Its P/B of -3.8 passed 'at or below book value' as a bargain, its ROE came out as -97%, and its P/E of 3.6 passed as good value. When equity is negative every equity ratio changes sign and stops meaning anything, so all of them now report a red flag saying why.")]));
children.push(bullet([B("Lenders. "), T("A bank funds itself with deposits, so its liabilities run to several times its equity by design, and it reports no current assets or liabilities. For a Financial Services company the liability, liquidity, quick-ratio and receivables checks return n/a with the reason, following the book's instruction to judge ratios against industry norms. Without this every bank would fail the solvency test.")]));
children.push(bullet([B("A flat margin. "), T("HDFC Bank's net margin went from 36.6% to 36.6% and was flagged as falling on a change in the third decimal. A change of under half a point now counts as unchanged.")]));
children.push(H3("Live, 18 October 2026 (latest annual report: year ending 31 March 2026)"));
children.push(table([1700, 1300, 1300, 1300, 3760], [
  ["Company", "Hallmarks met", "Profitability", "Solvency", "What stands out"],
  ["TCS", "4 of 6", "2 of 3", "2 of 2", "P/E 17.3 (inside the conservative band, not a bargain); trailing dividend down after a 2025 special"],
  ["HDFC Bank", "3 of 5", "1 of 2", "n/a (lender)", "One hallmark is not applicable to a bank"],
  ["MRPL", "3 of 6", "3 of 3", "0 of 2", "P/E 6.9, but liabilities 2.1x equity and a quick ratio of 0.39"],
  ["Vodafone Idea", "3 of 6", "1 of 3", "0 of 2", "Negative net equity: red flags, not a bargain"],
]));
children.push(P([B("One data caveat is printed on screen. "), T("The ratios are computed from yfinance's annual statement line items, which can differ from yfinance's own trailing figures and from other sites using standalone or last-four-quarter numbers: HDFC Bank's ROE is 8.9% from its annual statements (8.6% on year-end equity before Section 3.64's switch to average balances) against 13.8% in yfinance's info. The panel uses one source throughout and gives the fiscal year it read.")]));
children.push(H3("Ch. 3 and 4: the horizon, and the risks the forecast cannot see"));
children.push(P("The expander opens with what the book calls this holding. Mladjenovic counts anything under a year as short term, and holding a stock that briefly as trading or short-term speculating, not investing, because short-term prices are driven by what millions of other people do next. That is exactly the horizon every forecast here covers, so the note says plainly that the forecast reads the next few sessions and says nothing about the company."));
children.push(P("A second expander, Risks this forecast cannot see, lists Ch. 4's eight kinds of risk against what the app does about each. Only market risk and volatility is modelled (the cone, the risk panel and the stop rows). Financial risk is what the company check is for. Interest-rate, inflation, tax, political, personal and emotional risk are not modelled, and the table says so instead of leaving it implied. The book's advice for those closes the expander: learn before you buy, keep money you will need within a year out of stocks, and diversify."));
children.push(H3("Added: the payout ratio (Ch. 9) and the company's size (Ch. 1, 14)"));
children.push(P([B("Payout ratio. "), T("Dividends / earnings, under the Income (Ch. 9) group. The book: 60% or less very safe, 50-70% a good range, 80% the maximum acceptable, so a tick up to 70%, a warning to 80%, a red flag above. Computed on TOTALS (cash dividends paid / net income, same fiscal year), because per-share history can change basis after a bonus issue or a merger: in yfinance's history HDFC Bank's EPS halves between FY2023 and FY2024 while its profit rose 26%. Live: HDFC Bank 31%, MRPL 36% (a year earlier 624%, when its profit nearly vanished), and TCS 80.1%, a red flag by the book's rule read literally - an IT-services firm needs little capital and pays out most of what it earns. The rule is printed beside the result.")]));
children.push(P([B("Size. "), T("The book's cut-offs are in US dollars. NSE's equivalent is SEBI's rank-based classification (top 100 large, 101-250 mid, the rest small), and NSE's broad indices are built from it, so the band comes from the live Nifty 50 + Next 50, Midcap 150 and Smallcap 250 lists, with anything outside them labelled micro cap. The lists rebalance twice a year, so a band can lag a re-rating by a few months. For a small or micro cap the panel adds Ch. 14's two rules - in business at least three years, and net profits of 10% or more over two years or longer, read here as a net margin of at least 10% in each of the last two years - and its warning that buying before there is evidence of growth is speculating.")]));
children.push(table([2600, 2800, 3960], [
  ["Company", "Band", "Ch. 14 rules"],
  ["TCS", "Large cap (Nifty 50)", "-"],
  ["Vodafone Idea", "Large cap (Nifty Next 50)", "-"],
  ["MRPL", "Small cap (Smallcap 250)", "31 years listed (met); net margins 0.1% and 2.2% (short)"],
  ["Rain Industries", "Micro cap", "24 years (met); -3.7% and 0.3% (red flag)"],
  ["ideaForge", "Micro cap", "3 years (met); -38.6% and -7.5% (red flag)"],
]));
children.push(P("Not taken: Ch. 21's four judgement hallmarks (no data source; shown as questions); Ch. 16 screening tools (a screener ranks a whole cross-section: a different app, and 200+ statement fetches per screen); Ch. 12 company documents (annual-report reading is not automatable here); personal finance, brokers and US tax. The fetch is cached for six hours, and any failure leaves a one-line 'skipped' note rather than an error. 9 tests (test_stock_checklist.py)."));

children.push(H2("3.63 Trailing stops and targets (Mladjenovic, Stock Investing For Dummies, Ch. 17) - the book's 8% trailing stop is hit within a quarter 96% of the time"));
